The L 3 L^{3} -based strong Onsager theorem
vikram giri, hyunju kwon, and matthew novack

Abstract
In this work, we prove the L 3 L^{3} -based strong Onsager conjecture for the three-dimensional Euler equations. Our main theorem states that there exist weak solutions which dissipate the total kinetic energy, satisfy the local energy inequality, and belong to C t 0 ​ ( W 1 / 3 − , 3 ∩ L ∞ − ) C^{0}_{t}(W^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}-,3}\cap L^{\infty-}) . More precisely, for every β < 1 / 3 \upbeta<\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}} , we can construct such solutions in the space C t 0 ​ ( B 3 , ∞ β ∩ L 1 1 − 3 ​ β ) C^{0}_{t}(B^{\upbeta}_{3,\infty}\cap L^{\frac{1}{1-3\upbeta}}) .



1
Introduction

In this article, we consider the three-dimensional incompressible Euler equations

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u+(u\cdot\nabla)u+\nabla p=0\\
\mathrm{div}\,u=0\end{cases}

(1.1)

posed on the periodic domain
\mathbb{T}^{3}=[-\pi,\pi]^{3}
. These equations describe the evolution of an ideal volume-preserving fluid. The unknowns
u:\mathbb{T}^{3}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
p:\mathbb{T}^{3}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
represent the fluid velocity and the pressure, respectively. The first equation gives the balance of momentum and is derived from Newton’s second law of motion with internal force
-\nabla p
. The second equation, or divergence-free condition, encodes the incompressibility of the fluid.

Smooth solutions of the Euler equations satisfy the
local energy equality

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left(\frac{1}{2}|u|^{2}\right)+\mathrm{div}\left(\left(\frac{1}{2}|u|^{2}+p\right)u\right)=0\,,

(1.2)

which follows from taking the inner product of the momentum equation with the velocity. This identity asserts that the rate of change of kinetic energy in an arbitrary local region is balanced by the energy flux and the work done by the pressure through the boundary of the region. Integrating (
1.2
) in space, we see that the total kinetic energy is conserved in time:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}|u(\cdot,t)|^{2}dx=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}|u(\cdot,0)|^{2}dx,\qquad\forall\,t>0\,.

(1.3)

General weak solutions, however, need not satisfy (
1.2
). Instead, weak solutions
u\in L^{3}_{t,x}
are known to satisfy the following generalized local energy balance in the sense of distributions
[
22
]
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left(\frac{1}{2}|u|^{2}\right)+\mathrm{div}\left(\left(\frac{1}{2}|u|^{2}+p\right)u\right)=-D[u]\,.

(1.4)

The Duchon-Robert measure
D[u]
above is a space-time distribution measuring the dissipation due to possible singularities and is defined by the formula

D[u](x,t)=\lim_{\ell\to 0}\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\nabla\phi_{\ell}(z)\cdot\left(u(x+z,t)-u(x,t)\right)\left|u(x+z,t)-u(x,t)\right|^{2}\,dz\,,

(1.5)

where
\phi
is any even bump function with unit mass and
\phi_{\ell}(z)=\ell^{-3}\phi(\ell^{-1}z)
. In the case that
D[u]
is a non-negative distribution, then the weak solution
u
is said to satisfy the
local energy inequality

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left(\frac{1}{2}|u|^{2}\right)+\mathrm{div}\left(\left(\frac{1}{2}|u|^{2}+p\right)u\right)=-D[u]\leq 0\,.

(1.6)

It is straightforward to check that
D[u]\equiv 0
if there exists
\upbeta>\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
such that
u\in C^{0}_{t}B^{\upbeta}_{3,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
, where

\displaystyle\left\|v\right\|_{B_{p,\infty}^{\upbeta}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}=\left\|v\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}+\sup_{|z|>0}\frac{\left\|v(\cdot+z)-v(\cdot)\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}}{|z|^{\upbeta}}\,.

(1.7)

The following conjecture addresses the sharpness of this
\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
threshold.

Conjecture

(
L^{3}
-based strong Onsager conjecture).

Let
\upbeta\in(0,1)
and
T\in(0,\infty)
.

(a)

(Conservation and local energy equality)
For any
\upbeta>\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
, if a weak solution to the Euler equations belongs to
C^{0}([0,T];B^{\upbeta}_{3,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3}))
, then it satisfies the local energy equality (
1.2
) in the sense of distributions.

(b)

(Dissipation and local energy inequality)
For any
0<\upbeta<\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
, there exist weak solutions to the Euler equations belonging to
C^{0}([0,T];B^{\upbeta}_{3,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3}))
which satisfy the local energy inequality (
1.6
) in the sense of distributions and dissipate the total kinetic energy.

While the proof of part (
a
) is known from
[
22
]
, the best result to date for part (
b
) is due to De Lellis and the second author and treats the range
0<\upbeta<\nicefrac{{1}}{{7}}
. In this paper, we provide for the first time a proof of part (
b
) in the full range
0<\upbeta<\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
, via the following theorem.

Theorem 1.1

(
Dissipation and local energy inequality).

For any fixed
\upbeta\in(0,\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}})
there exist weak solutions to the Euler equations (
1.1
) which belong to
C^{0}([0,T];(B^{\upbeta}_{3,\infty}\cap L^{\frac{1}{(1-3\upbeta)}})(\mathbb{T}^{3}))
, satisfy the local energy inequality (
1.6
), and dissipate the total kinetic energy.

In the next subsection, we present the physical and mathematical motivation and history underlying the
L^{3}
-based strong Onsager conjecture.

1.1 Mathematical and physical background

We begin with the mathematical background, recalling that the Euler equations are formally related to the Navier-Stokes equations

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u^{\nu}+(u^{\nu}\cdot\nabla)u^{\nu}+\nabla p^{\nu}=\nu\Delta u^{\nu}\\
\mathrm{div}\,u^{\nu}=0\,\end{cases}

(1.8)

through the inviscid limit
\nu\to 0
(or the infinite Reynolds number limit after nondimensionalization). The well-known suitable weak solutions
u^{\nu}
of (
1.8
) are distributional solutions belonging to
L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})\cap L^{2}_{t}W^{1,2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
which satisfy

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left(\frac{1}{2}|u^{\nu}|^{2}\right)+\mathrm{div}\left(\left(\frac{1}{2}|u^{\nu}|^{2}+p^{\nu}\right)u^{\nu}-\nu\nabla\frac{1}{2}|u^{\nu}|^{2}\right)+\nu|\nabla u^{\nu}|^{2}\leq 0

(1.9)

in the sense of distributions
[
9
]
. Since the left-hand side is also equal to
-D[u^{\nu}]

[
22
]
, (
1.9
) is equivalent to the condition
D[u^{\nu}]\geq 0
. It is known that the weak solutions to (
1.8
) constructed by Leray
[
34
]
do indeed satisfy this sign condition
[
22
]
. Furthermore, if
u^{\nu}\rightarrow u
in the
L^{3}_{x,t}
topology, then
u
must be a weak solution to Euler satisfying the local energy inequality (
1.6
). Thus constructing Euler flows satisfying the local energy inequality allows for the possibility that such flows may be obtained as inviscid limits of suitable solutions of Navier-Stokes.

On the other hand, the
L^{3}
-based strong Onsager conjecture is closely related to the famous original conjecture of Onsager. Onsager observed that any weak solution of (
1.1
) in the space
L^{\infty}_{t}C^{\alpha}_{x}
with
\alpha>\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
must conserve the total kinetic energy as in (
1.3
), while for
\alpha<\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
, he conjectured that “turbulent dissipation as described could take place just as readily without the final assistance by viscosity”
[
39
]
. Remarkably, Onsager’s conjecture has been proven in the subsequent decades. The conservation statement in
L^{3}_{t}B^{\alpha}_{3,\infty}
for
\alpha>\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
was partially resolved by Eyink in
[
23
]
, fully proven by Constantin, E, and Titi
[
13
]
, and extended by Constantin, Cheskidov, Friedlander, and Shvydkoy
[
11
]
and Drivas and Eyink
[
21
]
. Conversely, Isett
[
27
]
provided the first construction of non-conservative solutions in
C^{\alpha}
for
\alpha<\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
, and Buckmaster, De Lellis, Székelyhidi, and Vicol extended Isett’s result to dissipative solutions
[
5
]
. The first such pathological constructions are due to Scheffer
[
40
]
and Shnirelman
[
41
]
, while the proofs in
[
27
]
and
[
5
]
build upon a series of partial results, including
[
26
,
4
,
14
]
, which are rooted in the seminal works of De Lellis and Székelyhidi in
[
16
,
19
]
. These works connected ideas of Nash
[
37
]
for isometric embeddings to fluid dynamics, and the technology stemming from
[
16
,
19
]
is known by the names “convex integration” or “Nash iteration.” We refer to the surveys
[
8
,
20
]
for a more complete history of the Onsager program.

Despite the success of the Onsager program, the
L^{3}
-based “strong” version of Onsager’s conjecture, or more generally any type of Onsager conjecture incorporating (
1.6
), remained open prior to this work. The previous best result is due to the second author and De Lellis
[
15
]
, who showed the existence of Hölder continuous weak solutions to the Euler equations in
C^{\upbeta}_{t,x}
for any
\upbeta<\nicefrac{{1}}{{7}}
which also satisfy the strict local energy inequality (
1.9
). We also refer to earlier results of De Lellis and Székelyhidi
[
17
]
and Isett
[
28
]
, the latter of which formulated the strong
C^{0}
Onsager conjecture, which posits for each
\upbeta<\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
(or even for
\upbeta=\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
) the existence of weak solutions satisfying the local energy inequality which belong to
L^{\infty}_{t}C^{\upbeta}_{x}
.

The
L^{3}
-based strong Onsager conjecture is also strongly motivated by the study of turbulent fluids in the inviscid limit
\nu\rightarrow 0
. The starting point for this discussion is the
zeroth law of turbulence
, which asserts that the mean energy dissipation rate per unit mass does not vanish in the limit
\nu\rightarrow 0
; more precisely,

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\to 0}\left\langle\nu|\nabla u^{\nu}|^{2}+D[u^{\nu}]\right\rangle=\varepsilon>0\,.

(1.10)

Here
\langle\cdot\rangle
denotes either a space-time average or an ensemble average. As a consequence of (
1.3
) and the zeroth law, one therefore expects to find weak solutions satisfying (
1.1
) in the sense of distributions, which however do not satisfy (
1.2
) or (
1.3
). While the zeroth law has been verified to a great degree in experiments (see discussion in
[
8
]
), and recent progress has been made in the context of the forced Navier-Stokes equations
[
1
,
2
]
, a full mathematical validation of the zeroth law remains a major open problem in fluid dynamics.

The
L^{3}
-based strong Onsager conjecture is connected as well to Kolmogorov’s 1941 phenomenological theory of turbulence
[
31
,
32
,
33
]
. Using the zeroth law and the additional assumptions that the statistics of turbulent flows are homogeneous, isotropic, and self-similar,
1
1

1

For a treatment of K41 based on these assumptions of restored symmetry, we refer to the book of Frisch
[
24
]
.
K41 theory offers predictions for the behavior of the
p^{\rm th}
-order absolute structure functions
S_{p}(\ell)
, defined by

\displaystyle S_{p}(\ell):=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{0}^{T}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}|u^{\nu}(x+\ell z,t)-u^{\nu}(x,t)|^{p}\,dx\,dz\,dt\,.

Specifically, K41-style asymptotic analysis predicts that for
\nu\ll 1
, and for length scales
\ell
within the inertial range,
S_{p}(\ell)
satisfies a scaling relation of the form
S_{p}(\ell)\sim|\ell|^{\zeta_{p}}
, where
\zeta_{p}=\nicefrac{{p}}{{3}}
. Recalling the Besov spaces
B^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}_{p,\infty}
defined in (
1.7
), this suggests the uniform boundedness of turbulent flows in the Besov space
B_{p,\infty}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}
.
The case
p=3
is special because the longitudinal variant
S^{\parallel}_{3}(\ell)
of the absolute structure function
S_{3}(\ell)
obeys a scaling relation known as Kolmogorov’s
\nicefrac{{4}}{{5}}
law, consistent with the prediction that
\zeta_{3}=1
. The
\nicefrac{{4}}{{5}}
law is strongly supported by experimental evidence
[
24
, Figure 8.8]
,
[
10
, Figure 5]
,
[
29
, Figure 3]
,
[
30
, Figure 1]
, indicating that
B^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}_{3,\infty}
is a natural function space for turbulent flows.

Our final motivating factor from the study of turbulence is the phenomenon of intermittency, which may be characterized as deviations from the K41 scaling
S_{p}(\ell)\sim\nicefrac{{p}}{{3}}
for
p\neq 3
. When
p<3
, one typically observes that
\nicefrac{{\zeta_{p}}}{{p}}>\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
, while for
p>3
, one typically observes that
\nicefrac{{\zeta_{p}}}{{p}}<\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
; see
[
24
, Figure 8.8]
, or
[
30
, Figure 6]
for a recent numerical simulation. These observations suggest that while
B^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}_{3,\infty}
is a physically reasonable space for turbulent flows, the Hölder space
C^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}
in which Onsager’s theorem has been proven may not be. In this direction, the third author and Vicol recently proved an intermittent Onsager theorem
[
38
]
for non-conservative solutions in
C^{0}_{t}(H^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}-}\cap L^{\infty-})\subset C^{0}_{t}B_{3,\infty}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}-}
, building upon earlier work of the third author with Buckmaster, Masmoudi, and Vicol
[
6
]
.

We close this subsection with a short discussion of the proof of Theorem
1.1
, upon which we expand in the next subsection. Our construction utilizes Nash iteration (convex integration) to generate a sequence of approximate solutions to the Euler equations with errors converging to zero over the course of the iteration. Standard Nash iterations adapted to the Euler equations measure approximate solutions in either
L^{2}
or
L^{\infty}
. Our
intermittent
L^{3}
iteration
, however, measures approximate solutions directly in
L^{3}
. Furthermore, the approximate velocities in standard Nash iterations are essentially partial Fourier sums, and the frequency separation of the velocity increments added at each step is crucial. This article however introduces a
wavelet-inspired scheme
that enables frequency overlap between successive velocity increments. Finally, in contrast to the straightforward construction of pressure in existing Nash iterations for the Euler equations, we must carefully construct an
intermittent pressure
, which plays an integral role throughout the proof.

1.2 Blueprint for an intermittent, wavelet-inspired scheme

In order to motivate an iteration which produces solutions in
C^{0}_{t}B^{\upbeta}_{3,\infty}
, we must identify the main difficulties in the iterations which produce solutions in Hölder spaces
C^{\beta}_{x,t}
. We recall that in
[
28
]
, Isett constructed
C_{x,t}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{15}}-}
weak solutions of 3D Euler as a limit of subsolutions
u_{q}
to the following system:

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u_{q}+\mathrm{div}\left(u_{q}\otimes u_{q}\right)+\nabla p_{q}=\mathrm{div}R_{q}\\
\partial_{t}\left(\frac{1}{2}|u_{q}|^{2}\right)+\mathrm{div}\left(\left(\frac{1}{2}|u_{q}|^{2}+p_{q}\right)u_{q}\right)\leq(\partial_{t}+u_{q}\cdot\nabla)\kappa_{q}+\mathrm{div}\varphi_{q}+\mathrm{div}\left(R_{q}u_{q}\right)\\
\mathrm{div}\,u_{q}=0\,,\end{cases}

(1.11)

where
(u\otimes v)_{ij}=u_{i}v_{j}
and
[\mathrm{div}(u\otimes v)]_{j}=\partial_{i}(u_{i}v_{j})
. The first equation is known as the Euler-Reynolds system, while the second one is called the relaxed local energy inequality. The Reynolds stress error
R_{q}
is a negative definite symmetric tensor,
\kappa_{q}=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\mathrm{tr\,}R_{q}
, and
\varphi_{q}
is a vector field called the current error. All three terms converge to zero as
q
goes to
\infty
in the sense of distributions, thus producing in the limit a weak solution
u
to 3D Euler which satisfies the local energy inequality. The functions
u_{q},R_{q},\varphi_{q}
are assumed to oscillate at spatial frequencies no larger than
\lambda_{q}\approx a^{(b^{q})}
, where
a
is sufficiently large and
b>1
is as small as possible. Abbreviating the mixed
L^{\infty}_{t}L^{p}_{x}
norms with simply
\|\cdot\|_{p}
, the natural inductive estimates for a
C_{x,t}^{\beta}
scheme are

\left\|u_{q}\right\|_{\infty}\lesssim 1\,,\qquad\left\|\nabla_{x}^{N}\nabla u_{q}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda_{q}^{-\beta+N+1}\,,\qquad\left\|\nabla_{x}^{N}R_{q}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda_{q+1}^{-2\beta}\lambda_{q}^{N}\,,\qquad\left\|\nabla_{x}^{N}\varphi_{q}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda_{q+1}^{-3\beta}\lambda_{q}^{N}\,.

(1.12)

Note that interpolating the first two bounds shows that
\{u_{q}\}_{q=1}^{\infty}
is uniformly bounded in the
C^{\beta}
norm. Then
w_{q+1}=u_{q+1}-u_{q}
is constructed to oscillate at frequency
\lambda_{q+1}\approx\lambda_{q}^{b}
and to cancel out the previous step stress/current errors through the following equations:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\leq\lambda_{q}}|w_{q+1}|^{2}w_{q+1}=-2\varphi_{q},\quad\mathbb{P}_{\leq\lambda_{q}}(w_{q+1}\otimes w_{q+1})=-R_{q}\,.

(1.13)

Eliding for the moment the fact that
R_{q}
and
\varphi_{q}
are not scalar-valued functions, we define the correction
w_{q+1}=w_{q+1,R}+w_{q+1,\varphi}
, similar to
[
28
]
, by

w_{q+1,\varphi}\approx\vartheta_{q+1,\varphi}(x_{2},x_{3})\vec{e}_{1}(-2\varphi_{q})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}\,,\quad w_{q+1,\varphi}\approx\vartheta_{q+1,R}(x_{2},x_{3})\vec{e}_{1}\left((-2\varphi_{q})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}\texttt{m}_{\varphi,2}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}+(-R_{q})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\right)\,,

using products of low-frequency functions and shear flows
\vartheta_{q+1,\varphi}
,
\vartheta_{q+1,R}
with concentrated frequency support
\approx\lambda_{q+1}
. Here
\texttt{m}_{\varphi,2}\sim 1
is the square mean
(2\pi)^{-3}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\vartheta_{q+1,\varphi}^{2}
of the high frequency function
\vartheta_{q+1,\varphi}
. We further impose that the high frequency function
\vartheta_{q+1,R}
has unit squared mean and zero cubic mean, while
\vartheta_{q+1,\varphi}
has a unit cubic mean, which essentially decouples the equations in (
1.13
). We thus have that the first equation in (
1.13
) involves only
w_{q+1,\varphi}
, which gives the desired
(-2\varphi_{q})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}
. After plugging
w_{q+1,\varphi}
into the second equation, we treat
-\mathbb{P}_{\leq\lambda_{q}}(w_{q+1,\varphi}\otimes w_{q+1,\varphi})
as another error to be corrected by
w_{q+1,R}
. Thus
w_{q+1,R}
produces a low frequency part
(-2\varphi_{q})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}\texttt{m}_{\varphi,2}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}+(-R_{q})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
in (
1.13
). One naturally assumes that to achieve the optimal regularity for the constructed solution, both terms in the low-frequency portion of
w_{q+1,R}
have the same size, as given by (
1.12
).

Then
w_{q+1}
satisfies

\left\|\nabla_{x}^{N}w_{q+1}\right\|_{\infty}\lesssim\left\|R_{q}\right\|_{\infty}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q+1}^{N}+\left\|\varphi_{q}\right\|_{\infty}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}\lambda_{q+1}^{N}\lesssim\lambda_{q+1}^{-\beta+N}\,.

Interpolating the bounds for
N=0,1
, we find that
w_{q+1}
has unit
C^{\beta}
norm, as did
u_{q}
. The new Reynolds stress will then include the error term
R_{q+1,\rm Nash}=\mathrm{div}^{-1}\left(w_{q+1}\cdot\nabla u_{q}\right)
(named after the analogous error term in Nash’s original isometric embedding iteration
[
37
]
), which can be estimated by

\displaystyle\big|\underbrace{\mathrm{div}^{-1}}_{\textnormal{gains $\lambda_{q+1}$}}(\underbrace{w_{q+1}}_{\lambda_{q+1}^{-\beta}}\cdot\underbrace{\nabla u_{q}}_{\approx\lambda_{q}^{1-\beta}})\big|

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{q+2}^{-2\beta}

\displaystyle\iff\lambda_{q}^{b(-1-\beta)+1-\beta+2\beta b^{2}}\leq 1\iff\beta(2b^{2}-b-1)

\displaystyle\leq b-1\iff\beta\leq\frac{1}{2b+1}\,.

Thus as
b\rightarrow 1
,
\beta\rightarrow\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
, as desired. However, the analogous error term in the local energy inequality, called the Nash current error, only satisfies the estimate

\displaystyle\big|\underbrace{\mathrm{div}^{-1}}_{\textnormal{gains $\lambda_{q+1}$}}(\underbrace{\mathbb{P}_{=\lambda_{q+1}}(w_{q+1}\otimes w_{q+1})}_{\lambda_{q+1}^{-2\beta}}\,:\,\underbrace{\nabla u_{q}}_{\approx\lambda_{q}^{1-\beta}})\big|

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{q+2}^{-3\beta}

\displaystyle\iff\lambda_{q}^{b(-1-2\beta)+1-\beta+3\beta b^{2}}\leq 1\iff\beta(3b^{2}-2b-1)

\displaystyle\leq b-1\iff\beta\leq\frac{1}{3b+1}\,.

(1.14)

This evident
\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}
regularity ceiling is also imposed by several similar current error terms. All the evidence from existing Nash iteration schemes indicates that the above heuristics cannot be improved. Furthermore, the best
C^{\alpha}
result to date suffers from further complications which limit the regularity to
C^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{7}}-}

[
15
]
, suggesting that
even in the most optimistic scenario, Nash iterations are incapable of reaching the
C^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}-}
threshold for the strong Onsager conjecture and can only reach the threshold
C^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}}
.

1.2.1 Intermittent
L^{3}
Nash iteration

The first difference between an intermittent Nash iteration and a homogeneous (opposite of intermittent) iteration is that the high-frequency homogeneous shear flows
\vartheta_{q+1,\varphi}\vec{e}_{1}
and
\vartheta_{q+1,R}\vec{e}_{1}
are replaced by a pair of
intermittent
shear flows
\varrho_{q+1,R}\vec{e}_{1}
and
\varrho_{q+1,\varphi}\vec{e}_{1}
(described in detail in the next subsubsection). Here ‘‘intermittent’’ means that different
L^{p}_{x}
norms satisfy very different bounds, i.e.

\left\|\nabla_{x}^{N}\varrho_{q+1,R}\right\|_{p}\lesssim r_{q}^{\frac{2}{p}-1}\lambda_{q+1}^{N}\,,\qquad\left\|\nabla_{x}^{N}\varrho_{q+1,\varphi}\right\|_{p}\lesssim r_{q}^{\frac{2}{p}-\frac{2}{3}}\lambda_{q+1}^{N}\,,\qquad\textnormal{for some $0<r_{q}\ll 1$}\,.

(1.15)

Then one naturally defines
w_{q+1}=w_{q+1,R}+w_{q+1,\varphi}
by

w_{q+1,\varphi}\approx\varrho_{q+1,\varphi}(-2\varphi_{q})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}\,,\qquad w_{q+1,R}\approx\varrho_{q+1,R}\left(r_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}(-2\varphi_{q})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}+(-R_{q})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\right)\,.

Unlike the
C^{0}
iteration, the low frequency in
w_{q+1,R}
has an additional smallness factor
\texttt{m}_{\varphi,2}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\sim r_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}
in front of
(2\varphi_{q})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}
, due to the intermittency of
\varrho_{q+1,\varphi}
. We now explain below that the flexibility afforded by the extra parameter
r_{q}
allows our solutions to exceed the
\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}
threshold described above. To see this, we must first recall that the iteration in
[
38
]
required a “Goldilocks amount” of intermittency
r_{q}=(\lambda_{q}\lambda_{q+1}^{-1})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
in order to produce a solution in
B^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}-}_{3,\infty}
; any larger or smaller choice of
r_{q}
causes the size of
\nabla w_{q+1}
to grow too quickly as
q\rightarrow\infty
. This value of
r_{q}
is determined by the ratio between the frequency of the error
\varphi_{q}
and the maximum frequency of the intermittent shear flow
\varrho_{q+1,\varphi}
associated to
w_{q+1,\varphi}
, raised to the power of
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
.
Rather remarkably, we shall see below that the Goldilocks amount of intermittency is
precisely
the minimum amount required in order to make the current Nash error estimate consistent with
B^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}-}_{3,\infty}
regularity.

We first interpolate the
L^{1}
and
L^{\infty}
inductive estimates for
R_{q}
and
L^{2}
and
L^{\infty}
inductive estimates for
\nabla u_{q}
from
[
38
]
to posit that

\left\|u_{q}\right\|_{3}\lesssim 1\ ,\qquad\left\|\nabla^{N}\nabla u_{q}\right\|_{3}\leq\lambda_{q}^{-\beta+N+1}r_{q-1}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}\,,\qquad\left\|\nabla_{x}^{N}R_{q}\right\|_{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}\leq\lambda_{q+1}^{-2\beta}\lambda_{q}^{N}\,,

for integers
N\geq 0
, where
\beta<\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
and
u_{q}\rightarrow u
in the
B^{\beta-}_{3,\infty}
topology. We write
\beta-
to emphasize that
r_{q-1}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}
incurs a small power loss
\lambda_{q-1}^{\frac{b-1}{6}}
which disappears as
b\rightarrow 1
. In order to make the low-frequency coefficient
\varphi_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}
in
w_{q+1,R}
no larger than
(-R_{q})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
, the natural inductive bound for
\varphi_{q}
is

\left\|\nabla^{N}\varphi_{q}\right\|_{1}\leq\lambda_{q+1}^{-3\beta}r_{q}^{-1}\lambda_{q}^{N}\,.

Combining this bound with the sharp
L^{p}
decoupling estimate proved in
[
25
, Lemma A.1]
and the extra factor of
r_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}
in the definition of
w_{q+1,R}
above yields the balanced estimates

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{N}w_{q+1,\varphi}\right\|_{3}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\varphi_{q}\right\|_{1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}\left\|\nabla^{N}\varrho_{q+1,\varphi}\right\|_{3}\approx\lambda_{q+1}^{-\beta+N}r_{q}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}\,,

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{N}w_{q+1,R}\right\|_{3}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\left\|R_{q}\right\|_{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}+r_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}\left\|\varphi_{q}\right\|_{1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}\right)\left\|\nabla^{N}\varrho_{q+1,R}\right\|_{3}\approx\lambda_{q+1}^{-\beta+N}r_{q}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}\,.

Now recalling the structure of
w_{q+1,\varphi}\approx\varrho_{q+1,\varphi}(-2\varphi_{q})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}
and using decoupling, Hölder’s inequality, and our estimates on
\varrho_{q+1,\varphi}
,
\varphi_{q}
, and
\nabla u_{q}
, we may estimate the Nash current error term corresponding to
w_{q+1,\varphi}
by

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{div}^{-1}\mathbb{P}_{=\lambda_{q+1}}(w_{q+1,\varphi}\otimes w_{q+1,\varphi})\,:\,\nabla u_{q}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\|\mathrm{div}^{-1}{\mathbb{P}_{=\lambda_{q+1}}(\varrho_{q+1,\varphi}^{2})}\big\|_{1}\big\||\varphi_{q}|^{\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}}\nabla u_{q}\big\|_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda_{q+1}^{-1}r_{q}^{\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}}\left\|\varphi_{q}\right\|_{1}^{\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}}\left\|\nabla u_{q}\right\|_{3}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda_{q+1}^{-1}r_{q}^{\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}}\lambda_{q+1}^{-2\beta}r_{q}^{-\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}}\lambda_{q}^{-\beta+1}r_{q-1}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}\,.

(1.16)

In order for this estimate to meet the desired inductive bound of
\lambda_{q+2}^{-3\beta}r_{q+1}^{-1}
, we see that we need

\displaystyle\lambda_{q+1}^{-1-2\beta}\lambda_{q}^{1-\beta}r_{q-1}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}\leq\lambda_{q+2}^{-3\beta}r_{q+1}^{-1}\qquad\underbrace{\impliedby}_{r_{q-1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}r_{q+1}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}>1}\qquad\lambda_{q+1}^{-1-2\beta}\lambda_{q}^{1-\beta}\leq\lambda_{q+2}^{-3\beta}r_{q+1}^{-\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}}

Note crucially that the inequality on the right has gained
r_{q+1}^{-\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}}
compared with (
1.14
). Then using that
r_{q+1}^{-1}=\lambda_{q}^{\frac{b(b-1)}{2}}
, the inequality on the right is equivalent to

\displaystyle\beta\left(3b^{2}-2b-b\right)\leq(b-1)\left(1+\frac{b}{3}\right)\qquad\iff\qquad\beta\leq\frac{1+\frac{b}{3}}{3b+1}\,,

so that
\beta\rightarrow\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
as
b\rightarrow 1
. Similar estimates hold for the Nash current error from
w_{q+1,R}
, as well as for a number of current error terms which faced
C^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}}
regularity limitations in the
C^{0}
iteration.

We conclude by noting that while the basic scaling considerations above indicate that an
L^{3}
iteration inspired by
[
38
]
has some hope, the techniques from
[
38
]
would suffer from a number of significant shortcomings if one were to attempt to use them in a proof of the strong
L^{3}
Onsager conjecture. We explain the most immediate of these shortcomings in the next subsubsection.

1.2.2 Partial wavelet sums

In order to understand the need for partial wavelet sums in our iteration, we begin by examining the consequences of replacing high-frequency shear flows
\vartheta_{q+1,R}\vec{e}_{1}
with high-frequency, intermittent shear flows
\varrho_{q+1,R}\vec{e}_{1}
. Intermittency in Nash iterations dates back to the work of Buckmaster and Vicol
[
7
]
for the 3D Navier-Stokes equations; we refer to
[
12
,
3
,
35
]
for further developments for the Navier-Stokes equations. The intermittent Mikado flows used in
[
38
]
were introduced by Modena and Székelyhidi in
[
36
]
(see also the homogeneous Mikado flows due to Daneri and Székelyhidi
[
14
]
). One should visualize the intermittent Mikado flows
\varrho_{q+1,\varphi}\vec{e}_{1}
or
\varrho_{q+1,R}\vec{e}_{1}
as shear flows supported in thin tubes of diameter
\lambda_{q+1}^{-1}
around lines in the
\vec{e}_{1}
direction, which have been periodized to scale
(\lambda_{q+1}r_{q})^{-1}
. The parameter
r_{q}=\lambda_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q+1}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
thus quantifies both the measure of the support and the
L^{p}
norms, and the effective frequencies are contained in the range
[\lambda_{q+1}r_{q},\lambda_{q+1}]=[(\lambda_{q}\lambda_{q+1})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}},\lambda_{q+1}]
. Thus we see that intermittency
smears out
the frequency support of
w_{q+1}
.

This smearing of frequencies greatly affects nonlinear errors such as the current oscillation error

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}^{-1}\circ\mathrm{div}\left(\varphi_{q}+(\mathbb{P}_{\leq\lambda_{q}}+\mathbb{P}_{>\lambda_{q}})(\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}|w_{q+1,\varphi}|^{2}w_{q+1,\varphi})\right)\approx-\mathrm{div}^{-1}\mathbb{P}_{\geq\lambda_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}\left(\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}|\varrho_{q+1,\varphi}|^{2}\varrho_{q+1,\varphi}\right)\vec{e}_{1}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{q}\,.

In the above approximate equality we have used the form of
w_{q+1,\varphi}=(-2\varphi_{q})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}\varrho_{q+1,\varphi}\vec{e}_{1}
, the identity
\vec{e}_{1}\cdot\nabla\varrho_{q+1,\varphi}\equiv 0
, and the heuristic that the leading order behavior of the operator
\mathrm{div}^{-1}
on a product of high and low frequency terms can be understood by simply applying it to the high frequency term. The maximum frequency of the error term above is
\lambda_{q+1}
, and the minimum frequency is
\lambda_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
. Then if we attempt to absorb this error term into
\varphi_{q+1}
, we see that

\displaystyle\big\|-\underbrace{\mathrm{div}^{-1}\mathbb{P}_{\geq\lambda_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}}_{\textnormal{gains $\lambda_{q}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q+1}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}$}}\underbrace{\left(\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}|\varrho_{q+1,\varphi}|^{2}\varrho_{q+1,\varphi}\right)\vec{e}_{1}}_{\textnormal{unit $L^{1}$ norm}}\cdot\underbrace{\nabla\varphi_{q}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\textnormal{$L^{1}$ size}\\
\lambda_{q+1}^{-3\beta}r_{q}^{-1}\end{subarray}}\big\|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{q+2}^{-3\beta}r_{q+1}^{-1}

\displaystyle\iff\lambda_{q}^{3\beta b^{2}-3\beta b+\frac{1}{2}(1-b)+\frac{1}{2}(1-b)(b-1)}\leq 1\iff\beta

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{6}\,.

Thus intermittency creates errors at frequencies
below

\lambda_{q+1}
which are too large to be absorbed into
\varphi_{q+1}
.

In
[
38
]
, the analogue of this issue in the Euler-Reynolds system was rectified by performing a further frequency decomposition of
[(\lambda_{q}\lambda_{q+1})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}},\lambda_{q+1}]
and adding further velocity increments, which still have maximum frequency
\lambda_{q+1}
, to handle the errors at frequencies lower than
\lambda_{q+1}
. Attempting such a strategy here leads one to define the
higher order current error

\varphi_{q,\alpha}
at frequency
\lambda_{q}^{1-\alpha}\lambda_{q+1}^{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in[\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}},1]
by

\varphi_{q,\alpha}:=-\mathrm{div}^{-1}\mathbb{P}_{\approx\lambda_{q}^{1-\alpha}\lambda_{q+1}^{\alpha}}\left(\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}|\varrho_{q+1,\varphi}|^{2}\varrho_{q+1,\varphi}\right)\vec{e}_{1}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{q}\,,

which would be corrected by a
higher order velocity increment

w_{q+1,\alpha,\varphi}
. Now let
R_{q,\alpha}
be the analogous
higher order stress error
as in
[
38
]
, which will be corrected by
w_{q+1,\alpha,R}
. As before, we then set

\displaystyle w_{q+1,\alpha,\varphi}\approx\varrho_{q+1,\alpha,\varphi}(-2\varphi_{q,\alpha})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}},\quad w_{q+1,\alpha,R}\approx\varrho_{q+1,\alpha,R}\left(r_{q,\alpha}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}(-2\varphi_{q,\alpha})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}+(-R_{q,\alpha})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\right)\,,

where
r_{q,\alpha}
is the intermittency parameter for
\varrho_{q+1,\varphi,\alpha}
.
To determine
r_{q,\alpha}
, we recall that
|\varphi_{q,\alpha}|^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}r_{q,\alpha}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}
must be no larger than
|R_{q,\alpha}|^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
. Assuming that all intermediate higher order errors are corrected as desired, the higher order errors
R_{q,\alpha}
and
\varphi_{q,\alpha}
with
\alpha\approx 1^{-}
will be absorbed to
R_{q+1}
and
\varphi_{q+1}
, respectively, leading to

\displaystyle\lambda_{q+2}^{-2\beta}\approx\left\|R_{q+1}\right\|_{L^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}}\approx\left\|R_{q,1^{-}}\right\|_{L^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}}\gtrsim\left\|\varphi_{q,1^{-}}\right\|_{L^{1}}^{\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}}r_{q,1^{-}}^{\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}}\approx\left\|\varphi_{q+1}\right\|_{L^{1}}^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}\approx\lambda_{q+2}^{-2\beta}r_{q+1}^{-\frac{2}{3}}r_{q,1^{-}}^{\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}}\implies r_{q,1^{-}}\lesssim r_{q+1}\ll 1\,.

This however stands in contradiction with the restriction that maximum frequency of
w_{q+1,\varphi}
is no larger than
\lambda_{q+1}
. This is due to the fact that the associated Mikado flows
\varrho_{q+1,\alpha,\varphi}
have effective frequency support
[\lambda_{q+1}r_{q,\alpha},\lambda_{q+1}]
, which must remain
above
the frequency
\lambda_{q}^{1-\alpha}\lambda_{q+1}^{\alpha}
of the error
\varphi_{q,\alpha}
, leading
r_{q,1^{-}}\approx 1
as in
[
38
]
.
The failure of the strategy from
[
38
]
suggests lifting the restriction on the maximum frequency of the higher order velocity increments.

To adjust the frequency support of
w_{q+1,\alpha,\varphi}
, we turn to the Goldilocks ratio that determines the maximum and minimum frequencies of the Mikado flow
\varrho_{q+1,\alpha,\varphi}
, for given frequency of the corrected error
\varphi_{q,\alpha}
. The Goldilocks ratio suggests setting the maximum frequency of
\varrho_{q+1,\varphi,1^{-}}
to be
\lambda_{q+2}
, and the minimum frequency to be
\lambda_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q+2}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
. The same reasoning also suggests that for general
\alpha
, one should set
r_{q,\alpha}\approx\left(\lambda_{q}^{1-\alpha}\lambda_{q+1}^{\alpha}\lambda_{q+1}^{1-\alpha}\lambda_{q+2}^{\alpha}\right)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
, so that the maximum frequency of
\varrho_{q+1,\alpha,\varphi}
is
\lambda_{q+1}^{1-\alpha}\lambda_{q+2}^{\alpha}
and the minimum is
\lambda_{q+1}^{1-\alpha}\lambda_{q+2}^{\alpha}r_{q,\alpha}
. Interestingly, one may view these choices as a restoration of self-similarity which had been broken by the scheme in
[
38
]
. Indeed the choice of
r_{q,\alpha}
from
[
38
]
implies that
w_{q+1,\alpha,R}
was
much
less intermittent than
w_{q+1,R}
as
\alpha\rightarrow 1
, thus breaking the intermittent self-similarity of the different components of the velocity field. The natural conclusion of these observations, which in some sense is validated by our analysis in this paper, is that
the local energy inequality imposes intermittent self-similarity by fixing the Goldilocks parameter of intermittency throughout the iteration
.

Before we delve into the consequences of adjusting the maximum frequency of velocity increments, let us first introduce a basic set-up for our iteration based on the discussion in the previous paragraphs. In this set-up, we treat the construction of
w_{q+1,\alpha,\varphi}
as a distinct iterative step rather than a sub-step of
q\to q+1
, because

w_{q+1,\alpha}
corrects
\varphi_{q,\alpha}
and
R_{q,\alpha}
in a manner completely analogous to how
w_{q+1}
corrected
\varphi_{q}
and
R_{q}
.
We assume the existence of a velocity field
u_{q}=\widehat{u}_{q}+(u_{q}-\widehat{u}_{q})
(where the “hat” notation is used to encode frequency information described below), a Reynolds stress
R_{q}
, a current error
\varphi_{q}
, a pressure
p_{q}
, and an intermittent pressure
\pi_{q}
which satisfy

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u_{q}+\mathrm{div}\left(u_{q}\otimes u_{q}\right)+\nabla p_{q}=\mathrm{div}\left(R_{q}-\pi_{q}\mathrm{Id}\right)\\
\partial_{t}\left(\frac{1}{2}|u_{q}|^{2}\right)+\mathrm{div}\left(\left(\frac{1}{2}|u_{q}|^{2}+p_{q}\right)u_{q}\right)\leq(\partial_{t}+\widehat{u}_{q}\cdot\nabla)\kappa_{q}+\mathrm{div}\varphi_{q}+\mathrm{div}\left((R_{q}-\pi_{q}\mathrm{Id})\widehat{u}_{q}\right)\\
\mathrm{div}\,u_{q}=0\,,\end{cases}

(1.17)

where
\kappa_{q}:=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\mathrm{tr\,}\left(R_{q}-\pi_{q}\mathrm{Id}\right)
. Note that we have substituted the negative definite symmetric tensor
R_{q}-\pi_{q}\mathrm{Id}
for
R_{q}
in (
1.11
). The role of the scalar function
\pi_{q}
will be discussed in subsection
1.2.4
. We assume the existence of a large parameter
\bar{n}
such that
2
2

2

In a standard Nash iteration,
\bar{n}=1
. For us
\bar{n}
will be a large positive integer which however is still fixed independently of
q
.

\widehat{u}_{q}
oscillates at spatial frequencies no larger than
\lambda_{q}
and
u_{q}-\widehat{u}_{q}
oscillates at spatial frequencies in between
\lambda_{q+1}
and
\lambda_{{q+\bar{n}}-1}
. In general, the subscript
q^{\prime}
with a “hat” (as in
\widehat{u}_{q^{\prime}}
) denotes a velocity field with maximum frequency
\lambda_{q^{\prime}}
, while the subscript
q^{\prime}
and no “hat” (as in
u_{q^{\prime}}
) denotes a velocity field with maximum frequency
\lambda_{q^{\prime}+\bar{n}-1}
. Choosing
\beta
close to
\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
, we then inductively assume that

\displaystyle\left\|u_{q}\right\|_{3}\lesssim 1\,,\qquad\left\|\nabla_{x}^{N}\nabla u_{q}\right\|_{3}\lesssim\lambda_{{q+\bar{n}}-1}^{-\beta+1+N}\qquad\iff\qquad\left\|\widehat{u}_{{q+\bar{n}}-1}\right\|_{3}\lesssim 1\,,\qquad\left\|\nabla_{x}^{N}\nabla\widehat{u}_{{q+\bar{n}}-1}\right\|_{3}\lesssim\lambda_{{q+\bar{n}}-1}^{-\beta+1+N}\,.

Next, the Reynolds stress
R_{q}
may decomposed as
R_{q}=\sum_{q^{\prime}=q}^{{q+\bar{n}}-1}R_{q}^{q^{\prime}}
, the intermittent pressure
\pi_{q}
may be decomposed as
\pi_{q}=\sum_{q^{\prime}=q}^{\infty}\pi_{q}^{q^{\prime}}
,
3
3

3

While the error terms stop at
{q+\bar{n}}-1
, or the highest active frequency of an error term, the pressure terms past
{q+\bar{n}}-1
are required so that the pressure satisfies certain scaling laws specified in subsubsection
2.4.2
. See also subsubsection
1.2.4
.
and the current error
\varphi_{q}
may be decomposed as
\varphi_{q}=\sum_{q^{\prime}}^{{q+\bar{n}}-1}\varphi_{q}^{q^{\prime}}
. The parameter
q^{\prime}
encodes the frequency
\lambda_{q^{\prime}}
at which
R_{q}^{q^{\prime}}
,
\varphi_{q}^{q^{\prime}}
, and
\pi_{q}^{q^{\prime}}
oscillate. We therefore assume that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{x}^{N}R_{q}^{q^{\prime}}\right\|_{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}+\left\|\nabla_{x}^{N}\pi_{q}^{q^{\prime}}\right\|_{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}\leq\lambda_{q^{\prime}+\bar{n}}^{-2\beta}\lambda_{q^{\prime}}^{N}\,,\qquad\left\|\nabla_{x}^{N}\varphi_{q}^{q^{\prime}}\right\|_{1}\leq\lambda_{q^{\prime}+\bar{n}}^{-3\beta}r_{q^{\prime}}^{-1}\lambda_{q^{\prime}}^{N}\,,

where
r_{q^{\prime}}=\lambda_{q^{\prime}+\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q^{\prime}+\bar{n}}^{-1}\approx(\lambda_{q^{\prime}}\lambda_{q^{\prime}+\bar{n}})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
.

We then construct
w_{q+1}=\widehat{w}_{{q+\bar{n}}}=u_{q+1}-u_{q}
using intermittent Mikado flows
\varrho_{{q+\bar{n}},R}
and
\varrho_{{q+\bar{n}},\varphi}
which have minimum frequency
\lambda_{q+\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}}
and maximum frequency
\lambda_{q+\bar{n}}
. Since
w_{q+1}
is used to correct errors at frequency
\lambda_{q}
, these choices adhere to the Goldilocks ratio of intermittency. Furthermore,
w_{q+1}
is used to correct
R_{q}^{q}-\pi_{q}^{q}\mathrm{Id}
and
\varphi_{q}^{q}
while leaving
R_{q}^{q^{\prime}}-\pi_{q}^{q^{\prime}}\mathrm{Id}
and
\varphi_{q}^{q^{\prime}}

intact
for
q^{\prime}>q
. The concept of correcting only the lowest frequency error term out of a large collection of error terms at many frequencies distinguishes our scheme from all existing schemes for fluid equations. The net result of adding
w_{q+1}
will be the creation of new stress and current errors, which will get sorted into bins between
\lambda_{q+1}
and
\lambda_{{q+\bar{n}}}
and added to
R_{q}^{q^{\prime}}
and
\varphi_{q}^{q^{\prime}}
to form
R_{q+1}
and
\varphi_{q+1}
. We emphasize that the terms in the partial sum
u_{q+1}=w_{q+1}+w_{q}+w_{q-1}+\dots
have overlap in frequency when
|q^{\prime}-q^{\prime\prime}|\leq\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}
, so that
u_{q+1}
is not a partial Fourier sum of the limiting solution. Rather, we consider it as a partial wavelet sum and will address the difficulties arising from frequency overlap with spatial support separation.
See Figure
1
for a schematic of the active frequencies of various functions.

R_{q}^{q}
,
\varphi_{q}^{q}
max. freq. of
w_{q}=\widehat{w}_{{q+\bar{n}}}
\lambda_{q}
