\lambda_{q+\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}}
min. freq. of
w_{q}=\widehat{w}_{{q+\bar{n}}}
max. freq. of
R_{q-1}^{q-1}
,
\varphi_{q-1}^{q-1}
max. freq. of
w_{q}=\widehat{w}_{{q+\bar{n}}-1}
\lambda_{q-1}
\lambda_{{q+\bar{n}}-1}
\lambda_{q+\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}-1}
min. freq. of
w_{q}=\widehat{w}_{{q+\bar{n}}-1}

Figure 1:
The top segment depicts the relevant frequencies at stage
q-1
. The most important frequencies are indicated with black line segments and correspond to the frequency of
R_{q-1}^{q-1}
and
\varphi_{q-1}^{q-1}
(which are being corrected), and the minimum and maximum frequency of the velocity increment
w_{q}=\widehat{w}_{{q+\bar{n}}-1}
(which corrects
R_{q-1}^{q-1})
,
\varphi_{q-1}^{q-1}
). Note that these three frequencies obey the Goldilocks ratio of intermittency. The green line segments correspond to frequencies of errors
R_{q-1}^{k}
,
\varphi_{q-1}^{k}
for
q\leq k\leq{q+\bar{n}}-2
which are being ignored for the moment. The bottom segment depicts the same frequencies, but at stage
q
.

1.2.3 Dodging techniques

The inductive set-up described above needs to be complemented with assumptions on spatial support, as well as a methodology for propagating such information throughout the iteration. To give an example of the kind of support properties we require, let us define the velocity increment
w_{q+1}=w_{q+1,R}+w_{q+1,\varphi}
by

\displaystyle w_{q+1,\varphi}=(-2\varphi_{q}^{q})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}\varrho_{{q+\bar{n}},\varphi}\,,\qquad w_{q+1,R}=\left(r_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}(-2\varphi_{q}^{q})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}+(R_{q}^{q}-\pi_{q}^{q}\mathrm{Id})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\right)\varrho_{{q+\bar{n}},R}\,,

(1.18)

where
\varrho_{{q+\bar{n}},\varphi}
and
\varrho_{{q+\bar{n}},R}
satisfy estimates identical to (
1.15
) after replacing
\lambda_{q+1}
with
\lambda_{q+\bar{n}}
and using the new definition of
r_{q}=\lambda_{q+\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}}\lambda_{{q+\bar{n}}}^{-1}
. Then
w_{q+1,\varphi}
and
w_{q+1,R}
satisfy identical estimates, namely

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{N}w_{q+1,\varphi}\right\|_{3}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\varphi_{q}\right\|_{1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}\left\|\nabla^{N}\varrho_{{q+\bar{n}},\varphi}\right\|_{3}\approx\lambda_{{q+\bar{n}}}^{-\beta+N}r_{q}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}\,,

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{N}w_{{q+\bar{n}},R}\right\|_{3}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\left\|R_{q}^{q}\right\|_{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}+r_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}\left\|\varphi_{q}^{q}\right\|_{1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}\right)\left\|\nabla^{N}\varrho_{{q+\bar{n}},R}\right\|_{3}\approx\lambda_{{q+\bar{n}}}^{-\beta+N}r_{q}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}\,.

Now consider the Nash error obtained from adding
w_{q+1,\varphi}
, which we may estimate
4
4

4

Note the inverse divergence gain of
\lambda_{q+\bar{n}}
, which is larger than the minimum frequency
\lambda_{q+\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}}
of
w_{q+1}
. One can test the validity of this estimate by computing the one-dimensional version, where
\mathrm{div}^{-1}
is simply integration.
by

\displaystyle\big\|\mathrm{div}^{-1}(\varrho_{{q+\bar{n}},\varphi}(\varphi_{q}^{q})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}\nabla u_{q})\big\|_{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\|\underbrace{\mathrm{div}^{-1}\varrho_{{q+\bar{n}},\varphi}}_{\textnormal{$L^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}$ size $\lambda_{q+\bar{n}}^{-1}r_{q}^{\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}}$}}\underbrace{(\varphi_{q}^{q})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}}_{\textnormal{$L^{3}$ size $\lambda_{q+\bar{n}}^{-\beta}r_{q}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}$}}\cdot\underbrace{\nabla\widehat{u}_{q}}_{\textnormal{$L^{3}$ size $\lambda_{q}^{-\beta+1}r_{q-\bar{n}}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}$}}\big\|_{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\big\|\underbrace{\mathrm{div}^{-1}\varrho_{{q+\bar{n}},\varphi}}_{\textnormal{$L^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}$ size $\lambda_{q+\bar{n}}^{-1}r_{q}^{\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}}$}}\underbrace{(\varphi_{q}^{q})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}}_{\textnormal{$L^{3}$ size $\lambda_{q+\bar{n}}^{-\beta}r_{q}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}$}}\cdot\underbrace{\left(\nabla u_{q}-\nabla\widehat{u}_{q}\right)}_{\textnormal{$L^{3}$ size $\lambda_{{q+\bar{n}}-1}^{-\beta+1}r_{q-1}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}$}}\big\|_{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}\,.

Since this error term oscillates at frequency
\lambda_{{q+\bar{n}}}
, we expect its size to be
\lambda_{q+2\bar{n}}^{-2\beta}
(the analogue of
\delta_{q+2}
from
[
38
]
, for example). After a bit of arithmetic, one may check that the first term satisfies a sharp estimate when
\beta\rightarrow\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
(analogous to
\delta_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q}\lambda_{q+1}^{-1}\leq\delta_{q+2}
from a
C^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}-}
iteration, which is the size of the Nash error). The second term, however, is far too large, due to the fact that
\nabla u_{q}-\nabla\widehat{u}_{q}
has much larger
L^{3}
norm than
\nabla\widehat{u}_{q}
. The only way to close the estimate for the Nash error is then if

\mathrm{supp\,}w_{q+1}\cap\mathrm{supp\,}\left(u_{q}-\widehat{u}_{q}\right)=\emptyset\qquad\impliedby\mathrm{supp\,}w_{q+1}\cap\left(\mathrm{supp\,}\widehat{w}_{q+1}\cup\mathrm{supp\,}\widehat{w}_{q+2}\dots\cup\mathrm{supp\,}\widehat{w}_{{q+\bar{n}}-1}\right)=\emptyset\,,

where we have recalled that our “hat” notation gives that
u_{q}-\widehat{u}_{q}=\widehat{w}_{q+1}+\widehat{w}_{q+2}+\dots+\widehat{w}_{{q+\bar{n}}-1}
.

There is however a clear obstruction to this assertion. Consider the velocity increments
\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}}
defined analogously to (
1.18
) for
q+1\leq q^{\prime}\leq q+\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}
. These velocity increments are constructed using intermittent Mikado flows
\varrho_{q^{\prime},R}
and
\varrho_{q^{\prime},\varphi}
which have pipe spacing
\lambda_{q^{\prime}-\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}}^{-1}
and pipe thickness
\lambda_{q^{\prime}}^{-1}
. Since the thickness of these pipes is
larger
than the spacing of the pipes we plan to use at step
q
, namely
\lambda_{q+\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}}^{-1}
, there is no way to arrange the support of
\widehat{w}_{{q+\bar{n}}}
to be disjoint from the support of
\widehat{w}_{q+1},\dots\widehat{w}_{q+\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}}
without some adjustments to the definition of
\widehat{w}_{q+n}
. We have solved this issue through the creation of a new type of multi-scale, intermittent building block, which we call an
intermittent Mikado bundle
.

An intermittent Mikado bundle
\mathbb{B}_{\varphi}
or
\mathbb{B}_{R}
is a stationary solution of Euler given as a product of two intermittent pipe flows (for example with velocity parallel to
e_{1}
) such as

\mathbb{B}_{\varphi}(x,y):=e_{1}\varrho_{{q+\bar{n}},\varphi}(x_{2},x_{3})\widetilde{\varrho}_{q+1}(x_{2},x_{3})\,.

(1.19)

Here we have that
\varrho_{{q+\bar{n}},\varphi}
has minimum frequency
\lambda_{q+\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}}
and maximum frequency
\lambda_{{q+\bar{n}}}
, exactly as described in the last subsection. However,
\widetilde{\varrho}_{q+1}
is essentially a standard, non-intermittent Mikado flow with minimum and maximum frequency
\lambda_{q+1}
(so that the frequency support of
\mathbb{B}_{\varphi}
is essentially the same as that of
\varrho_{{q+\bar{n}},\varphi}
). We refer to
\widetilde{\varrho}_{q+1}
as the
bundling pipe
. The purpose of
\widetilde{\varrho}_{q+1}
is to enable the support of
\widehat{w}_{q+\bar{n}}
to be chosen disjointly from the supports of
\widehat{w}_{q+1},\dots,\widehat{w}_{q+\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}}
, all of which are comprised of pipes with thickness
less
than
\lambda_{q+1}^{-1}
and spacing
more
than
\lambda_{q+1}^{-1}
. This disjointness is ensured by
shifting
the supports of the bundling pipes. The idea that a compactly supported building block enjoys a degree of freedom according to shifts was first utilized in
[
15
]
and further developed using intermittency in
[
6
,
38
]
. The new development relative to the strategy used in
[
38
]
is the introduction of the bundling pipe, combined with inductive assumptions on the support of previous velocity increments. As in
[
6
]
and
[
38
]
, each bundle is pre-multiplied by space-time cutoff functions
\{a_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
which form a partition of unity and localize the bundles to a particular space-time region. The timescale on which a cutoff function
a_{i}
is non-zero is inversely proportional to the Lipschitz norm of
\widehat{u}_{q}
on the support of
a_{i}
. The machinery for these cutoff functions was developed in
[
6
]
and
[
38
]
. Finally, on the support of each
a_{i}
, we compose with a local flow map
\Phi_{q,i}
of
\widehat{u}_{q}
. Then a more accurate description of
w_{q+1,\varphi}
would be

w_{q+1,\varphi}=\sum_{i}\left(-2\varphi_{q}^{q}\right)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}a_{i}\left(\nabla\widehat{u}_{q}\right)\mathbb{B}_{i,\varphi}\circ\Phi_{q,i}\,.

(1.20)

For a pictorial depiction of the support of
\mathbb{B}_{i,\varphi}
, we refer to Figure
2
.

\small 4\lambda^{-1}_{q+1}
\small\lambda^{-1}_{q+1}
\small\lambda^{-1}_{q+\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}}
\small\lambda^{-1}_{{q+\bar{n}}}

Figure 2:
Depicted here is the cross-section of the support of a collection of intermittent pipe bundles
\mathbb{B}_{i,\varphi}
(not drawn to scale). The large circles represent the support of various
\varrho_{q+1,i}
’s. Each of these is shifted to ensure disjointness of
\widehat{w}_{{q+\bar{n}}}
with
\widehat{w}_{q+1},\dots,\widehat{w}_{q+\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}}
. The small circles represent the support of of various
\varrho_{{q+\bar{n}},i,\varphi}
’s. Each of these is shifted to ensure disjointness of
\widehat{w}_{{q+\bar{n}}}
with
\widehat{w}_{q+\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}+q},\dots,\widehat{w}_{{q+\bar{n}}-1}
.

The final piece of our dodging technology is needed to handle the material derivatives of nonlinear error terms such as

-\mathrm{div}^{-1}\mathbb{P}_{\neq 0}\mathrm{div}\left(\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}|w_{q+\bar{n},\varphi}|^{2}w_{q+\bar{n},\varphi}\right)\,.

Since
w_{{q+\bar{n}},\varphi}
is defined using intermittent Mikado bundles with active frequencies in between
\lambda_{q+\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}}
and
\lambda_{{q+\bar{n}}}
, we must divide this error term up according to the frequencies in this range. A standard Littlewood-Paley decomposition
\sum_{q^{\prime}=q+\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}}^{{q+\bar{n}}}\mathbb{P}_{\approx\lambda_{q^{\prime}}}
would however destroy any useful spatial support properties of these error terms. As a few simple heuristic computations show (similar to those made above for the Nash error), the material derivative
D_{t,{q^{\prime}-1}}=\partial_{q}+\widehat{u}_{q^{\prime}-1}\cdot\nabla
of the component of this error term at frequency
\lambda_{q^{\prime}}
would be too large unless the error is disjoint from the velocity increments
\widehat{w}_{q+1},\dots,\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}-1}
. In order to ensure this disjointness, we replace the standard Littlewood-Paley decomposition with a
synthetic Littlewood-Paley
decomposition
\sum_{q^{\prime}=q+\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}}^{{q+\bar{n}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\approx\lambda_{q^{\prime}}}
. This decomposition uses kernels that are compactly supported in space, so that the support of
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\approx\lambda_{q^{\prime}}}
is contained inside a ball of radius
\approx\lambda_{q^{\prime}}^{-1}
around the support of the input. Combined with the spatially localized inverse divergence developed in
[
6
]
and
[
38
]
, we therefore have that

\mathrm{supp\,}\widehat{w}_{k}\,\cap\,\mathrm{supp\,}\left[\mathrm{div}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\approx\lambda_{q^{\prime}}}\mathrm{div}\left(\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}|w_{{q+\bar{n}},\varphi}|^{2}w_{{q+\bar{n}},\varphi}\right)\right]\qquad\textnormal{for $q+1\leq k\leq q^{\prime}-1$}\,.

Then we can upgrade the natural material derivative estimate for this error term, corresponding to
D_{t,q}
, to
D_{t,q^{\prime}-1}=D_{t,q}+\left(\widehat{w}_{q+1}+\dots+\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}-1}\right)\cdot\nabla
,
for free
. It is simple to check that the synthetic Littlewood-Paley decomposition obeys essentially the same properties as a standard Littlewood-Paley decomposition with respect to integration (inverse divergence) and differentiation. We have checked these simple computations in the companion paper
[
25
, section 4.3]
and recall them here in the appendix.

1.2.4 Intermittent pressure

The primary purpose of our intermittent pressure
\pi_{q}^{q}
is to ensure that
\pi_{q}^{q}\mathrm{Id}-R_{q}^{q}
is positive-definite, identical to the role of the pressure decrement
p_{q}-p_{q+1}
in previous
C^{0}
Nash iterations for the Euler equations. In
C^{0}
schemes, for example,
\pi_{q}^{q}=2\left\|R_{q}^{q}\right\|_{C^{0}}
is typically chosen to be constant in space, as in either the Onsager theorem
[
27
,
5
]
or the partial results
[
28
,
15
]
for the strong
C^{0}
Onsager conjecture. In the intermittent iteration from
[
38
]
,
\pi_{q}^{q}
was chosen essentially as
2|R_{q}^{q}|
and added into the system at step
q+1
. This strategy is acceptable because the pressure adjustment does not contribute to the new error
R_{q+1}
. However, it will not work for the relaxed local energy inequality; as we will explain, this is because we must
invert the divergence
on a current error term containing
\pi_{q}^{q}
, which requires quite a bit of structure.

To illustrate this point, notice that when we add
u_{q+1}-u_{q}=w_{q+1}=\widehat{w}_{{q+\bar{n}}}
into (
1.17
), one of the error terms in the relaxed local energy inequality will be

(\partial_{t}+u_{q}\cdot\nabla)\left(|w_{q+1}|^{2}+\kappa_{q}^{q}\right)=(\partial_{t}+\widehat{u}_{q}\cdot\nabla)\left(|w_{q+1}|^{2}+\kappa_{q}^{q}\right)\,,

where
\kappa_{q}^{q}=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\mathrm{tr\,}(R_{q}^{q}-\pi_{q}^{q}\mathrm{Id})
. Note that we have used
dodging
to assert that
(u_{q}-\widehat{u}_{q})\cdot\nabla|w_{q+1}|^{2}=0
.
The presence of
\pi_{q}^{q}
arises from its prior construction at
step
q
and subsequent integration into the relaxed system. Consequently,
\kappa_{q}^{q}

cancels out the low frequency portion of
|w_{q+1}|^{2}
, leaving only a high-frequency remainder for which one can invert the divergence. We remark that without the presence of
\kappa_{q}^{q}
,
(\partial_{q}+\widehat{u}_{q}\cdot\nabla)|w_{q+1}|^{2}
is quite threatening since
w_{q+1}
may be
increasing
the energy along the trajectories of
\widehat{u}_{q}
in some localized region. Referring to (
1.20
), we see that this will indeed be the case in the regions where

(\partial_{t}+\widehat{u}_{q}\cdot\nabla)|w_{q+1,R}|^{2}=(\partial_{t}+\widehat{u}_{q}\cdot\nabla)\left(\left(r_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}(-2\varphi_{q}^{q})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}+(R_{q}^{q}-\pi_{q}^{q}\mathrm{Id})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\right)^{2}a_{i}^{2}(\nabla\widehat{u}_{q})\right)|\mathbb{B}_{i,R}|^{2}\circ\Phi_{q,i}>0\,.

(1.21)

Due to the fact that
\Phi_{q,i}
is only coherent on a timescale determined by the local Lipschitz norm of
\nabla\widehat{u}_{q}
, we must “turn off” the
\mathbb{B}_{i,R}
bundle after some time has passed; in other words,
D_{t,q}(a_{i}^{2})<0
in some region of space. But since the
a_{i}
’s form a partition of unity, that means that there exists some
i^{\prime}
such that
D_{t,q}(a_{i^{\prime}}^{2})>0
in that region of space. Therefore this term cannot be expected to have the correct sign in general, and so
\kappa_{q}^{q}
serves an essential role.

The intermittent pressure
\pi_{q}^{q}
associated to
R_{q}^{q}
, however, is not solely generated at step at step
q
. Instead, its components have been progressively formed in preceding steps and carried over through inductive steps.
To understand the idea, let us consider the construction of a new intermittent pressure
\pi_{q+1}^{q+\bar{n}}
, which guarantees the positive-definiteness of
\pi_{q+1}^{q+\bar{n}}\mathrm{Id}-R_{q+1}^{q+\bar{n}}
. Following
[
38
]
, a straightforward choice is
\pi_{q+1}^{q+\bar{n}}=2|R_{q+1}^{q+\bar{n}}|
. Incorporating this new intermittent pressure
\pi_{q+1}^{q+\bar{n}}
to the Euler-Reynold equations adds
-2|R_{q+1}^{q+\bar{n}}|
to a new pressure increment
p_{q+1}-p_{q}
, which subsequently generates a new error in the relaxed local energy inequality,

\displaystyle-2(\partial_{t}+\widehat{u}_{q}\cdot\nabla)|R_{q+1}^{q+\bar{n}}|.

We first remark that handling such an error term in
C^{0}
schemes is relatively simple because the pressure increment can be chosen to be constant in space, as discussed ealier. In an intermittent scheme, however, this term is more complex due to the smearing of the frequency support of
R_{q+1}^{q+\bar{n}}
.
Indeed, it will contain quite a large range of active frequencies which contribute nontrivially to the amplitude of
|R_{q+1}^{q+\bar{n}}|
. While we shall give a heuristic explanation of the active frequencies in
|R_{q+1}^{q+\bar{n}}|
below, we emphasize that any low frequency portions will likely have to be added in previous steps and carried inductively until they are needed.

To this end, we analyze
|R_{q+1}^{q+\bar{n}}|
. For simplicity, imagine that
R_{q+1}^{q+\bar{n}}
only contains the nonlinear error term
\mathrm{div}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\approx\lambda_{q+\bar{n}}}\mathrm{div}(w_{q+1}\otimes w_{q+1})
,
and set
w_{q+1}=(R_{q}^{q})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathbb{B}_{q+\bar{n}}
where
\mathbb{B}_{q+\bar{n}}
is a scalar-valued,
L^{2}
-normalized intermittent function with maximum frequency
\lambda_{q+\bar{n}}
. With these simplifications, we have

|R_{q+1}^{q+\bar{n}}|\approx\big|\underbrace{\nabla R_{q}^{q}}_{\textnormal{freq. $\lambda_{q}$}}\underbrace{\mathrm{div}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\approx\lambda_{q+\bar{n}}}\mathbb{B}_{q+\bar{n}}^{2}}_{\textnormal{freq. $\lambda_{q+\bar{n}}$}}\big|=\underbrace{\left|\nabla R_{q}^{q}\right|\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{=0}\left|\mathrm{div}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\approx\lambda_{q+\bar{n}}}\mathbb{B}_{q+\bar{n}}^{2}\right|}_{=\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\lesssim\lambda_{q}}|R_{q+1}^{q+\bar{n}}|}+\underbrace{\left|\nabla R_{q}^{q}\right|\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\neq 0}\left|\mathrm{div}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\approx\lambda_{q+\bar{n}}}\mathbb{B}_{q+\bar{n}}^{2}\right|}_{=\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{[\lambda_{q+\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}},\lambda_{q+\bar{n}}]}|R_{q+1}^{q+\bar{n}}|}\,.

(1.22)

As we see, the leading order portion of
R_{q+1}^{q+\bar{n}}
is composed of both low and high frequency components, but taking the magnitude of the high frequency part generates a low frequency component. Decomposing the magnitude further, we notice that the last term of (
1.22
) exhibits a similar structure with the stress errors arising at step
q+1
, and its material derivative can be absorbed to new current errors.
The second-to-last term of (
1.22
), on the other hand, is essentially a rescaled version of
|R_{q}^{q}|
, which is
therefore
inductive
in nature.
Repeating the analysis above under the simplification
R_{q^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime}}\approx\nabla R_{q^{\prime}-\bar{n}}^{q^{\prime}-\bar{n}}\mathrm{div}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\approx\lambda_{q^{\prime}}}\mathbb{B}_{q^{\prime}}^{2}
, we obtain a wavelet decomposition of
|R_{q+1}^{q+\bar{n}}|
as

\displaystyle|R_{q+1}^{q+\bar{n}}|\approx\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{[\lambda_{q+\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}},\lambda_{q+\bar{n}}]}|R_{q+1}^{q+\bar{n}}|+\frac{\lambda_{q}}{\lambda_{q+\bar{n}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{[\lambda_{q-\frac{\bar{n}}{2}},\lambda_{q}]}|R_{q}^{q}|+\frac{\lambda_{q-\bar{n}}}{\lambda_{q}}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{[\lambda_{q-\frac{3}{2}\bar{n}},\lambda_{q-\bar{n}]}}|R_{q-\bar{n}}^{q-\bar{n}}|+\cdots\,,

where we terminate the decomposition after finitely many steps, at which the last term of the sum is no longer intermittent and bounded uniformly in space.
Since the term
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{[\lambda_{q+\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}},\lambda_{q+\bar{n}}]}|R_{q+1}^{q+\bar{n}}|
is handled at step
q+1
, the pressure
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{[\lambda_{q^{\prime}-\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}},\lambda_{q^{\prime}}]}|R_{q^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime}}|
must be added at the same step that
R_{q^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime}}
is created. We then summarize that when we produce a stress error of the form
R_{q^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime}}
, we not only add the pressure
|\nabla R_{q^{\prime}-\bar{n}}^{q^{\prime}-\bar{n}}|\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\neq 0}|\mathrm{div}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\approx\lambda_{q^{\prime}}}\mathbb{B}_{q^{\prime}}^{2}|
at the same step of iteration, but also add rescaled
5
5

5

In practice, due to the presence of other errors in
R_{q}^{q}
, the rescaling factor will be raised to the power of
2\beta
; see (
2.25
).
copies that appear in wavelet decomposition of future stress errors. We refer to these rescaled pressure terms needed for future wavelet decompositions as
anticipated pressure
; see the discussion in subsection
3.2.4
. In general, the collection of all pieces of pressures that are constructed for
R_{q+1}^{k}
as above at multiple steps
forms a
intermittent pressure

\pi_{q+1}^{k}
.

Finally, we note that at a technical level, we will construct intermittent pressure
\pi_{q}^{k}
associated to
R_{q}^{k}
so that
\pi_{q}^{k}\lambda_{k}^{N}
dominates
|\nabla_{x}^{N}R_{q}^{k}|
for
N\rightarrow\infty
as
\beta\rightarrow\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
. In fact, we will construct
\pi_{q}^{k}
to dominate material derivatives of
R_{q}^{k}
, spatial and material derivatives of
\varphi_{q}^{k}
and
\nabla\widehat{u}_{k}
, and even spatial and material derivatives of
itself
. We refer to these bounds as
pointwise bounds
, and the net effect is that in fact almost every estimate in this manuscript can be reduced to a pointwise bound in terms of an intermittent pressure
\pi_{q}^{k}
, save for the
L^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}
and
L^{\infty}
norms of
\pi_{q}^{k}
itself. The fact that the pressure plays such a fundamental role in every estimate of this paper contrasts very sharply with every convex integration scheme for fluid equations to date, in which the pressure plays a more passive role.

1.3 Organization

Lastly, we provide a brief summary of the contents covered in the subsequent sections. Section
2
lists the inductive hypotheses, introduces the inductive statement in Proposition
2.11
, and presents the proof of Theorem
1.1
assuming Proposition
2.11
.

The proof of Proposition
2.11
is then contained in sections
3
–
6
. In particular, section
3
first sets up the inductive proof by defining the intermittent Mikado bundles and cutoff functions needed for the definition of the velocity increment, before defining and estimating the velocity increment and its potentials. In addition, section
3
defines and estimates most of the new Reynolds stress errors, the transport and current Nash errors (which are closely related to the new Reynolds stress errors), the associated pressure increments, and the mollification errors. The proofs of the assertions in this section are contained in
[
25
]
, as they bear some similarities to the constructions in
[
6
]
and
[
38
]
. Next, section
4
introduces the new dodging techniques for intermittent Mikado bundles, which form a crucial component of the wavelet-based scheme. Section
5
then considers the main new components of the current error generated by adding the new velocity increment
w_{q+1}
to the relaxed local energy inequality. We define and estimate both the current errors and associated pressure increments in this section. The final substantive portion of the proof of Proposition
2.11
is contained in section
6
. Here we gather all pressure increments constructed in the previous sections and define a new pressure increment and a new intermittent pressure, which are shown to satisfy the proper inductive assumptions. We then finalize the definition of all stress and current errors to conclude the proof of the inductive proposition.

Finally, section
7
specifies parameter choices and delineates useful inequalities resulting from these choices. Then in Appendix
A
, we collect the abstract technical components needed throughout the paper.

2
Inductive proposition and proof of the main theorem

The main goal of this section is to state the inductive proposition and prove the main theorem; this is the content of subsection
2.7
. However, we must first introduce a number of notations in subsection
2.1
, before listing the inductive assumptions in subsections
2.2
–
2.6
. Specifically, subsection
2.2
introduces the relaxed equations, subsection
2.3
introduces the inductive cutoff functions, subsection
2.4
introduces the intermittent pressure, subsection
2.5
introduces the inductive “dodging” hypotheses, and subsection
2.6
introduces the inductive velocity bounds. Throughout subsections
2.2
–
2.6
, we identify precisely where in the manuscript each inductive assumption is proven. We shall assume that all inductive assumptions at the
q^{\textnormal{th}}
step hold on the domain
[-\tau_{q-1},T+\tau_{q-1}]\times\mathbb{T}^{3}
.

2.1 General notations and parameters

We start by introducing the primary parameters

\beta\,,\bar{n}\,,b\,,\lambda_{q}\,,\delta_{q}\,,r_{q}\,,\Gamma_{q}\,,\varepsilon_{\Gamma}

which appear in the inductive hypotheses. First, we choose an
L^{3}
-based regularity index
\beta\in[\nicefrac{{1}}{{7}},\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}})
. Next, we fix
\bar{n}\in 6\mathbb{N}
(used to describe active frequencies in certain inductive objects; see (
1.17
) and the subsequent discussion) such that

\beta<\frac{1}{3}\cdot\frac{\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{3}}}{\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{3}}+2}-\frac{2}{\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{3}}+2}\,,\qquad\beta<\frac{2}{3}\cdot\frac{\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}-1}{\bar{n}}\,.

(2.1)

Next, we choose
b\in(1,\nicefrac{{25}}{{24}})
close to
1
such that

\displaystyle\beta<\frac{1}{3b^{\bar{n}}}\cdot\frac{1+b+\dots+b^{\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{3}}-1}}{1+b+\dots+b^{\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{3}}+1}}

\displaystyle-\frac{2\left(1+(b-1)(1+\cdots+b^{\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}-1})^{2}\right)}{1+b+\dots+b^{\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{3}}+1}}\,,\quad\frac{2}{3b^{\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}}}\cdot\frac{1+\dots+b^{\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}-2}}{1+\dots+b^{\bar{n}-1}}

(2.2a)

\displaystyle b^{\bar{n}}<2

\displaystyle\,,\qquad\frac{(b^{\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}-1}+\dots+b+1)^{2}}{b^{\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}-1}+\dots+b+1}(b-1)<(b-1)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,.

(2.2b)

We now define the frequency parameter
\lambda_{q}
, the amplitude parameter
\delta_{q}
, the intermittency parameter
r_{q}
, and the multi-purpose parameter
\Gamma_{q}
by

\displaystyle\lambda_{q}=2^{\left\lceil(b^{q})\log_{2}a\right\rceil}\approx a^{(b^{q})}\,,\qquad\delta_{q}=\lambda_{q}^{-2\beta}\,,

(2.3)

\displaystyle r_{q}=\frac{\lambda_{q+\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}}\Gamma_{q}}{\lambda_{q+\bar{n}}}

\displaystyle\,,\qquad\Gamma_{q}=2^{\left\lceil\varepsilon_{\Gamma}\log_{2}\left(\frac{\lambda_{q+1}}{\lambda_{q}}\right)\right\rceil}\approx\left(\frac{\lambda_{q+1}}{\lambda_{q}}\right)^{\varepsilon_{\Gamma}}\approx\lambda_{q}^{(b-1)\varepsilon_{\Gamma}}\,.

(2.4)

The large positive integer
a
and the small positive number
0<\varepsilon_{\Gamma}\ll(b-1)^{2}<1
are defined in (
xiii
) and (
i
) of subsection
7.1
, respectively. Note that the intermittency parameter
r_{q}
is determined by the “
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
rule” as in
[
38
]
.

We now introduce further parameters

\tau_{q}\,,\Lambda_{q}\,,\mathrm{T}_{q}\,,\mathsf{C_{\infty}}\,.

As described in the introduction, we often decompose
u_{q}=\widehat{u}_{q}+(u_{q}-\widehat{u}_{q})
. One may imagine that the gradient of velocity
\nabla\widehat{u}_{q^{\prime}}
will have spatial derivative cost
\approx\lambda_{q^{\prime}}
and
L^{3}
norm
\approx\tau_{q^{\prime}}^{-1}\approx\delta_{q^{\prime}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}r_{q^{\prime}-\bar{n}}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}\lambda_{q^{\prime}}
. It will in fact be necessary to slightly adjust the definition of
\tau_{q}^{-1}
using the parameter
\Lambda_{q}
(slightly larger than
\lambda_{q}
), which accounts for small spatial frequency losses due to mollification, and introduce a parameter
\mathrm{T}_{q}^{-1}
(much larger than
\tau_{q}^{-1})
, which accounts for temporal frequency losses due to mollification. We set

\displaystyle\lambda_{q}<\Lambda_{q}={\lambda_{q}\Gamma_{q}^{10}}\,,\qquad\tau_{q}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q}r_{q-\bar{n}}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}\Gamma_{q}^{35}\ll\mathrm{T}_{q}^{-1}\,,

(2.5)

and refer to (
7.5
) for the precise definition of
\mathrm{T}_{q}
. For the
L^{\infty}
norm of
R_{q}^{q}
(and other inductive objects), we use the parameter
\mathsf{C_{\infty}}
, which will satisfy (we refer to (
7.2
) for the precise choice of
\mathsf{C_{\infty}}
)

\displaystyle\lambda_{q}^{\frac{1}{\bar{n}}}\lesssim\Gamma_{q}^{\mathsf{C_{\infty}}}\lesssim\lambda_{q}^{\frac{12}{\bar{n}}}\,.

Finally, we will inductively propagate spatial and material derivative estimates, where we use the notation and paramaters

\displaystyle D_{t,q}=\partial_{t}+(\widehat{u}_{q}\cdot\nabla)\,,\qquad\mathsf{N}_{\rm cut,t}\,,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}}\,,\mathsf{N}_{\rm ind}\,,\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}\,.

The integers
\mathsf{N}_{\bullet}
above quantify the number of spatial and material derivative estimates propagated inductively and satisfy the ordering (see subsection
7.1
for the precise choices)

\displaystyle 1\ll\mathsf{N}_{\rm cut,t}\ll\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}}\ll\mathsf{N}_{\rm ind}\ll\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}\,.

In particular,
\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}}
helps us keep track of both sharp and lossy material derivative estimates. For this purpose, we use the following notation, which roughly says that “the first
N_{*}
material derivatives cost
\tau^{-1}
, while additional derivatives cost
\mathrm{T}^{-1}
.’’ We also list a few other notations in the subsequent two remarks.

Remark 2.1

(
Geometric upper bounds with two bases).

For all
n\geq 0
, we define

\mathcal{M}\left(n,N_{*},\tau^{-1},\mathrm{T}^{-1}\right):=\tau^{-\min\{n,N_{*}\}}\mathrm{T}^{-\max\{n-N_{*},0\}}\,.

Remark 2.2

(
Space-time norms).

In the remainder of the paper, we shall always measure objects using uniform-in-time norms
\sup_{t\in[T_{1},T_{2}]}\|\cdot(t)\|
, where
\|\cdot(t)\|
is any of a variety of norms used to measure functions defined on
\mathbb{T}^{3}\times[T_{1},T_{2}]
but restricted to time
t
. In a slight abuse of notation, we shall always abbreviate these space-time norms with simply
\|\cdot\|
.

Remark 2.3

(
Space-time balls).

For any set
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}
, we shall use the notations

\displaystyle B(\Omega,\lambda^{-1})

\displaystyle:=\left\{(x,t)\,:\,\exists\,(x_{0},t)\in\Omega\textnormal{ with }|x-x_{0}|\leq\lambda^{-1}\right\}

(2.6a)

\displaystyle B(\Omega,\lambda^{-1},\tau)

\displaystyle:=\left\{(x,t)\,:\,\exists\,(x_{0},t_{0})\in\Omega\textnormal{ with }|x-x_{0}|\leq\lambda^{-1}\,,|t-t_{0}|\leq\tau\right\}

(2.6b)

for space and space-time neighborhoods of
\Omega
of radius
\lambda^{-1}
in space and
\tau
in time, respectively.

2.2 Relaxed equations

We assume that there exists a given
q
-independent continuous function
E=E(t,x)\geq 0
such that the approximate solution
(u_{q},p_{q},R_{q},\varphi_{q},-\pi_{q})
at the
q^{\textnormal{th}}
step satisfies the Euler-Reynolds system

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u_{q}+\mathrm{div}(u_{q}\otimes u_{q})+{\nabla p_{q}}=\mathrm{div}(R_{q}-\pi_{q}\mathrm{Id})\\
\mathrm{div}\,u_{q}=0\end{cases}

(2.7)

and the relaxed local energy identity with dissipation measure
E
6
6

6

The dissipation measure
E
is the Duchon-Robert measure of the limiting solution.

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left(\frac{1}{2}|u_{q}|^{2}\right)+\mathrm{div}\left(\left(\frac{1}{2}|u_{q}|^{2}+p_{q}\right)u_{q}\right)=(\partial_{t}+\widehat{u}_{q}\cdot\nabla)\kappa_{q}+\mathrm{div}((R_{q}-\pi_{q}\mathrm{Id})\widehat{u}_{q})+\mathrm{div}\varphi_{q}-E\,.

(2.8)

In the above equation, we have set
\kappa_{q}=\nicefrac{{\mathrm{tr\,}(R_{q}-\pi_{q}\mathrm{Id})}}{{2}}
, and we use the decomposition and notations

u_{q}=\underbrace{\widehat{u}_{q-1}+\widehat{w}_{q}}_{=:\widehat{u}_{q}}+\widehat{w}_{q+1}+\dots+\widehat{w}_{q+\bar{n}-1}=:\widehat{u}_{q+\bar{n}-1}\,

(2.9)

for the velocity field. The stress error
R_{q}
has a decomposition

\displaystyle R_{q}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=q}^{q+\bar{n}-1}R_{q}^{k}\,.

(2.10)

where each
R_{q}^{k}
is a symmetric matrix. The pressure
\pi_{q}
has a decomposition

\displaystyle\pi_{q}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=q}^{{\infty}}\pi_{q}^{k}\,.

(2.11)

Similarly, the current error
\varphi_{q}
has a decomposition

\displaystyle\varphi_{q}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=q}^{q+\bar{n}-1}\varphi_{q}^{k}\,.

(2.12)

The Reynolds stress
R_{q}
and current error
\varphi_{q}
defined above will have frequency support in modes no larger than
\lambda_{q+\bar{n}-1}
(effectively speaking). We correct the errors which live at frequencies no higher than
\lambda_{q}
, denoted by
R^{q}_{q}
and
\varphi_{q}^{q}
, respectively. More generally, we denote the portions of
R_{q}
and
\varphi_{q}
with spatial derivative cost
\lambda_{k}
by
R_{q}^{k}
and
\varphi_{q}^{k}
, respectively. We verify the inductive assumptions in this section in Lemma
6.10
.

2.3 Inductive assumptions for velocity cutoff functions

Given the intermittent nature of the velocity vector field
\widehat{u}_{q^{\prime}}
, the cost of its associated material derivative
D_{t,q^{\prime}}
can vary significantly across different level sets of the gradient of velocity. To address this issue, we introduce velocity cutoff functions
\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}
, which are defined inductively. The cutoffs
\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}
partition the domain into distinct level sets of
\nabla\widehat{u}_{q^{\prime}}
, where
|\nabla\widehat{u}_{q^{\prime}}|\approx\tau_{q^{\prime}}^{-1}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i}
on
\mathrm{supp\,}\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}
. We first record here the key properties, including a partition of unity property, maximum value
{i_{\rm max}}
needed to index the cutoffs, derivative estimates, and Lebesgue norms. The local
L^{\infty}
estimates for velocity increment
\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}}
and velocity
\widehat{u}_{q^{\prime}}
, obtained as a consequence of the definition of
\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}
, can be found in subsection
2.6
. The concrete construction of
\psi_{i,q+\bar{n}}
of (
2.13
)–(
2.19
) for
q\mapsto q+1
(i.e.,
q^{\prime}=q+\bar{n}
) is contained in
[
25
, section 9]
, and the verification of the inductive assumptions here is contained as well in
[
25
, section 9]
and recalled in Proposition
3.24
. Finally, we note that all assumptions in subsection
2.3
are assumed to hold for all
q-1\leq q^{\prime}\leq q+\bar{n}-1
.

First, we assume that the velocity cutoff functions form a partition of unity:

\displaystyle\sum_{i\geq 0}\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}^{6}\equiv 1,\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}\psi_{i^{\prime},q^{\prime}}=0\quad\textnormal{for}\quad|i-i^{\prime}|\geq 2\,.

(2.13)

Second, we assume that there exists an
{i_{\rm max}}={i_{\rm max}}(q^{\prime})\geq 0
, bounded uniformly in
q^{\prime}
, such that

\displaystyle{i_{\rm max}}(q^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathsf{C_{\infty}}+12}{(b-1)\varepsilon_{\Gamma}}\,,

(2.14)

\displaystyle\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}\equiv 0\quad\mbox{for all}\quad i>{i_{\rm max}}(q^{\prime})\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\mbox{and}\qquad\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{{i_{\rm max}}(q^{\prime})}\leq\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-\bar{n}}^{\nicefrac{{\mathsf{C_{\infty}}}}{{2}}+18}\delta_{q^{\prime}}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}r_{q^{\prime}-\bar{n}}^{-\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}}\,.

(2.15)

For all
0\leq i\leq{i_{\rm max}}
, we assume the following pointwise derivative bounds for the cutoff functions
\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}
. First, for mixed space and material derivatives and multi-indices
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{k}
,
k\geq 0
,
0\leq|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}
, we assume that

\displaystyle\frac{{\bf 1}_{\mathrm{supp\,}\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}}}{\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}^{1-(K+M)/\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}}}\left|\left(\prod_{l=1}^{k}D^{\alpha_{l}}D_{t,q^{\prime}-1}^{\beta_{l}}\right)\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}\right|\leq\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}(\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}\lambda_{q^{\prime}})^{|\alpha|}\mathcal{M}\left(|\beta|,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}}-\mathsf{N}_{\rm cut,t},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}^{i+3}\tau_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}\mathrm{T}_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}\right)\,.

(2.16)

Next, with
\alpha,\beta,k
as above,
N\geq 0
and
D_{q^{\prime}}:=\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}}\cdot\nabla
, we assume that

\displaystyle\frac{{\bf 1}_{\mathrm{supp\,}\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}}}{\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}^{1-(N+K+M)/\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}}}\left|D^{N}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{k}D_{q^{\prime}}^{\alpha_{l}}D_{t,q^{\prime}-1}^{\beta_{l}}\right)\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}(\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}\lambda_{q^{\prime}})^{N}(\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i-5}\tau_{q^{\prime}}^{-1})^{|\alpha|}\mathcal{M}\left(|\beta|,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}}-\mathsf{N}_{\rm cut,t},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}^{i+3}\tau_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}\mathrm{T}_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}\right)

(2.17)

for
0\leq N+|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}
. Moreover, for
0\leq i\leq{i_{\rm max}}(q^{\prime})
, we assume the
L^{1}
bound

\displaystyle\left\|\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}\right\|_{1}\leq\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{-3i+{\mathsf{C}_{b}}}\qquad\mbox{where}\qquad{\mathsf{C}_{b}}=\frac{6+b}{b-1}\,.

(2.18)

Lastly, we assume that local timescales dictated by velocity cutoffs at a fixed point in space-time are decreasing in
q
. More precisely, for all
q^{\prime}\leq q+\bar{n}-1
and all
q^{\prime\prime}\leq q^{\prime}-1
, we assume

\psi_{i^{\prime},q^{\prime}}\psi_{i^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime\prime}}\not\equiv 0\quad\implies\quad\tau_{q^{\prime}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{-i^{\prime}}\leq\tau_{q^{\prime\prime}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime\prime}}^{-i^{\prime\prime}-25}\,.

(2.19)

This will be useful when we upgrade material derivatives from
D_{t,q^{\prime\prime}}
to
D_{t,q^{\prime}}
.

2.4 Inductive bounds on the intermittent pressure

The intermittent pressure
\pi_{q}
is designed to give pointwise control on errors and velocity increments. Towards this end, we shall use the phrase ‘‘pointwise estimates’’ to refer to such bounds on stress errors, current errors, or velocities in terms of various
\pi
’s. We introduce estimates for
\pi_{q}
in subsection
2.4.1
and establish precise relations between the intermittent pressure and errors/velocity increments in subsection
2.4.3
. (The
L^{p}
estimates of the errors will follow consequently.) Furthermore, as we discussed in the introduction, the intermittent pressure is constructed to anticipate the low-frequency parts of future pressure increments. We record the relevant properties in subsection
2.4.2
. All inductive assumptions appearing in subsection
2.4
will be verified for
q\mapsto q+1
in Section
6
.

2.4.1
L^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}
,
L^{\infty}
, and pointwise bounds for
\pi_{q}^{k}

We assume that for
q\leq k\leq q+\bar{n}-1
and
N+M\leq 2\mathsf{N}_{\rm ind}
,
\pi_{q}^{k}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|\psi_{i,k-1}D^{N}D_{t,k-1}^{M}\pi_{q}^{k}\right\|_{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{q}\Gamma_{k}\delta_{k+\bar{n}}\Lambda_{k}^{N}\mathcal{M}\left(M,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{k-1}^{i}\tau_{k-1}^{-1},\mathrm{T}_{k-1}^{-1}\right)\,.

(2.20a)

\displaystyle\left\|\psi_{i,k-1}D^{N}D_{t,k-1}^{M}\pi_{q}^{k}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{q}{\Gamma_{k}^{\mathsf{C_{\infty}}+1}}\Lambda_{k}^{N}\mathcal{M}\left(M,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{k-1}^{i}\tau_{k-1}^{-1},\mathrm{T}_{k-1}^{-1}\right)\,,

(2.20b)

\displaystyle\left|\psi_{i,k-1}D^{N}D_{t,k-1}^{M}\pi_{q}^{k}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{q}{\Gamma_{k}}\pi_{q}^{k}\Lambda_{k}^{N}\mathcal{M}\left(M,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{k-1}^{i}\tau_{k-1}^{-1},\mathrm{T}_{k-1}^{-1}\right)\,.

(2.20c)

For
q+\bar{n}\leq k\leq q+\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{pr}}-1
and
N+M\leq 2\mathsf{N}_{\rm ind}
, we assume that
\pi_{q}^{k}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|\psi_{i,q{+\bar{n}-1}}D^{N}D_{t,q{+\bar{n}-1}}^{M}\pi_{q}^{k}\right\|_{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{q}\Gamma_{{k}}\delta_{k+\bar{n}}\Lambda_{q+\bar{n}-1}^{N}\mathcal{M}\left(M,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{{q+\bar{n}-1}}^{i}\tau_{{q+\bar{n}-1}}^{-1},\mathrm{T}_{q+\bar{n}-1}^{-1}\right)

(2.21a)

\displaystyle\left\|\psi_{i,q{+\bar{n}-1}}D^{N}D_{t,q{+\bar{n}-1}}^{M}\pi_{q}^{k}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{q}\Gamma_{{q+\bar{n}-1}}^{\mathsf{C_{\infty}}+1}\Lambda_{q+\bar{n}-1}^{N}\mathcal{M}\left(M,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{q+\bar{n}-1}^{i}\tau_{q+\bar{n}-1}^{-1},\mathrm{T}_{q+\bar{n}-1}^{-1}\right)\,,

(2.21b)

\displaystyle\left|\psi_{i,q+\bar{n}-1}D^{N}D_{t,q+\bar{n}-1}^{M}\pi_{q}^{k}\right|

\displaystyle\leq{\Gamma_{q}}\pi_{q}^{k}\Lambda_{q+\bar{n}-1}^{N}\mathcal{M}\left(M,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{q+\bar{n}-1}^{i}\tau_{q+\bar{n}-1}^{-1},\mathrm{T}_{q+\bar{n}-1}^{-1}\right)\,.

(2.21c)

The bounds in (
2.20
) and (
2.21
) are verfied in Lemma
6.6
.

2.4.2 Lower and upper bounds for
\pi_{q}^{k}

For
k\geq q
, we assume that
\pi_{q}^{k}
has the lower bound

\displaystyle\pi_{q}^{k}\geq\delta_{k+\bar{n}}\,.

(2.22)

For all
q+\bar{n}-1\leq k^{\prime}<k\leq q+\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{pr}}-1
(see (
7.6
) for the definition of
\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{pr}}
), we assume that
\pi_{q}^{k}
has the upper bound

\displaystyle\pi_{q}^{k}\leq\pi_{q}^{k^{\prime}}\,.

(2.23)

For all
k\geq q+\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{pr}}
, we assume that

\pi_{q}^{k}\equiv\Gamma_{k}\delta_{k+\bar{n}}\,.

(2.24)

We finally assume that for all
q\leq q^{\prime}<q^{\prime\prime}<\infty
,

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{q^{\prime\prime}+\bar{n}}}{\delta_{q^{\prime}+\bar{n}}}\pi_{q}^{q^{\prime}}

\displaystyle<2^{q^{\prime}-q^{\prime\prime}}\pi_{q}^{q^{\prime\prime}}\,,\qquad\text{if}\,q+\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}\leq q^{\prime\prime}

(2.25a)

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{q^{\prime\prime}+\bar{n}}}{\delta_{q^{\prime}+\bar{n}}}\pi_{q}^{q^{\prime}}

\displaystyle<\pi_{q}^{q^{\prime\prime}}\,,\qquad\text{otherwise}\,.

(2.25b)

This final bound says that the
\pi_{q}^{k}
’s obey a scaling law which may be roughly translated as “any
\pi_{q}^{k+m}
for
m>0
can be bounded from below by an appropriately rescaled
\pi_{q}^{k}
.” All the bounds in this subsubsection are verified in Lemma
6.7
.

2.4.3 Pointwise bounds for errors, velocities, and velocity cutoffs

We assume that we have the pointwise estimates

\displaystyle\left|\psi_{i,k-1}D^{N}D_{t,k-1}^{M}R^{k}_{q}\right|

\displaystyle<\Gamma_{q}\Gamma_{k}^{-8}\pi^{k}_{q}\Lambda_{k}^{N}\mathcal{M}\left(M,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{k-1}^{i+20}\tau_{k-1}^{-1},\mathrm{T}_{k-1}^{-1}\Gamma_{k-1}^{10}\right)\,,

(2.26a)

\displaystyle\left|\psi_{i,k-1}D^{N}D_{t,k-1}^{M}{\varphi_{q}^{k}}\right|

\displaystyle<\Gamma_{q}\Gamma_{k}^{-12}(\pi^{k}_{q})^{\frac{3}{2}}r_{k}^{-1}\Lambda_{k}^{N}\mathcal{M}\left(M,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{k-1}^{i+20}\tau_{k-1}^{-1},\mathrm{T}_{k-1}^{-1}\Gamma_{k-1}^{10}\right)\,,

(2.26b)

\displaystyle\left|\psi_{i,k-1}D^{N}D_{t,k-1}^{M}\widehat{w}_{k}\right|

\displaystyle<\Gamma_{q}r_{k-\bar{n}}^{-1}(\pi_{q}^{k})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}{\Lambda}_{k}^{N}\mathcal{M}\left(M,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{k-1}^{i}\tau_{k-1}^{-1},\mathrm{T}_{k-1}^{-1}{\Gamma_{k-1}^{2}}\right)

(2.26c)

for
q\leq k\leq q+\bar{n}-1
, where the first bound holds for
N+M\leq 2\mathsf{N}_{\rm ind}
, the second bound holds for
N+M\leq\nicefrac{{\mathsf{N}_{\rm ind}}}{{4}}
, and the third bound holds for
N+M\leq\nicefrac{{3\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}}}{{2}}
. The first and second bounds above are verified in Lemma
6.14
, where we also verify the bound given below in (
2.28
). The proof of (
2.26c
) is given in Lemma
6.8
, where we also verify the bound given below in (
2.29
).

Remark 2.4

(
L^{p}
estimates on Reynolds errors from pointwise estimates).

The estimates on
R^{k}_{q}
in (
2.26a
) and the estimates on
\pi_{q}^{k}
in (
2.20
) imply that for
q\leq k\leq q+\bar{n}-1
and
N+M\leq 2\mathsf{N}_{\rm ind}
,
R_{q}^{k}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|\psi_{i,k-1}D^{N}D_{t,k-1}^{M}R_{q}^{k}\right\|_{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{q}^{2}\Gamma_{k}^{-7}\delta_{k+\bar{n}}\Lambda_{k}^{N}\mathcal{M}\left(M,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{k-1}^{i+20}\tau_{k-1}^{-1},\mathrm{T}_{k-1}^{-1}\Gamma_{q}^{10}\right)\,,

(2.27a)

\displaystyle\left\|\psi_{i,k-1}D^{N}D_{t,k-1}^{M}R_{q}^{k}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{q}^{2}\Gamma_{k}^{-7}{\Gamma_{k}^{\mathsf{C_{\infty}}}}\Lambda_{k}^{N}\mathcal{M}\left(M,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{k-1}^{i+20}\tau_{k-1}^{-1},\mathrm{T}_{k-1}^{-1}\Gamma_{q}^{10}\right)\,.

(2.27b)

While the main
L^{p}
estimates on the Reynolds stress follow from the pointwise estimates in terms of the pressure, we are forced to assume that
R_{q}^{k}
has a decomposition
R_{q}^{k}=R_{q}^{k,l}+R_{q}^{k,*}
, where
R_{q}^{k,*}
satisfies the stronger bound

\displaystyle\left\|D^{N}D_{t,k-1}^{M}R_{q}^{k,*}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\Gamma_{q}^{2}\mathrm{T}_{k}^{2\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}}}\delta_{k+2\bar{n}}\Lambda_{k}^{N}\mathcal{M}\left(M,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\tau_{k-1}^{-1},\mathrm{T}_{k-1}^{-1}\right)

(2.28)

for all
N+M\leq 2\mathsf{N}_{\rm ind}
. The extra superscript
l
stands for “local,” in the sense that
R_{q}^{k,l}
is a stress error over which we maintain control of the spatial support (see Hypothesis
2.8
), whereas
\ast
refers to non-local terms which are negligibly small. The reader can safely ignore such non-local error terms.

Finally, we assume that for all
q\leq q^{\prime}\leq q+\bar{n}-1
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{{i_{\rm max}}}\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}^{2}\delta_{q^{\prime}}r_{q^{\prime}-\bar{n}}^{-\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{2i}

\displaystyle\leq{2^{q-q^{\prime}}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}{r_{q^{\prime}-\bar{n}}^{-2}}\pi_{q}^{q^{\prime}}\,.

(2.29)

Using the heuristic that
|\nabla\widehat{u}_{q^{\prime}}|\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{supp\,}\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}}\approx\tau_{q^{\prime}}^{-1}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i}
and (
2.5
), this inequality says that
|\nabla\widehat{u}_{q^{\prime}}|^{2}\leq r_{q^{\prime}-\bar{n}}^{-2}\lambda_{q^{\prime}}^{2}\pi_{q}^{q^{\prime}}
.

2.5 Dodging inductive hypotheses

In this subsection, we list ‘‘dodging’’ inductive hypotheses, which encode the spatial support information which is crucial to our wavelet-inspired scheme. As discussed in the introduction, one component of this is dodging between velocity increments, which is detailed in Hypothesis
2.5
. To construct a new velocity increment with such dodging, it is necessary to keep a record of the density of previous velocity increments, as stated in Hypothesis
2.6
. Stronger statements than Hypotheses
2.5
and
2.6
will be proved in Section
4
(see Lemma
4.5
). As byproducts of the careful construction of the velocity increments, we also have Hypothesis
2.8
and Hypothesis
2.9
, which present dodging properties between velocity increments and stress errors/intermittent pressure. These bounds will be utilized later in the stress current estimate discussed in subsection
5.4
. Hypothesis
2.8
for
q\mapsto q+1
has been already verified in
[
25
, Lemma 8.16]
and is recalled in Lemma
3.29
. Lastly, Hypothesis
2.9
for
q\mapsto q+1
will be verified in subsection
6.2
. We formulate these hypotheses using the notation from Remark
2.3
.

Hypothesis 2.5

(
Effective dodging).

For
q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime}\leq q+\bar{n}-1
that satisfy
0<|q^{\prime\prime}-q^{\prime}|\leq\bar{n}-1
, we have that

B\left(\mathrm{supp\,}\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}},\lambda_{q^{\prime}}^{-1}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}+1}\right)\cap B\left(\mathrm{supp\,}\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime\prime}},\lambda_{q^{\prime\prime}}^{-1}\Gamma_{q^{\prime\prime}+1}\right)=\emptyset\,.

(2.30)

Hypothesis 2.6

(
Density of old pipe bundles
)
.

There exists a
q
-independent constant
\mathcal{C}_{D}
such that the following holds. Let
\bar{q}^{\prime},\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}
satisfy
q\leq\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}<\bar{q}^{\prime}\leq q+\bar{n}-1
, and set
7
7

7

The reasoning behind the choice of
d(\bar{q}^{\prime},\bar{q}^{\prime\prime})
is as follows. The set should be small enough that it can be contained in the support of a single
\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}
velocity cutoff. Since these functions oscillate at frequencies no larger than
\approx\lambda_{q^{\prime\prime}}
, the first number inside the minimum ensures that this is the case. The set should also be no larger than the size of a periodic cell for pipes of thickness
\bar{q}^{\prime}
, which is ensured by the second number inside the minimum.

d(\bar{q}^{\prime},\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}):=\min\left[(\lambda_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}\Gamma_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}^{7})^{-1},(\lambda_{\bar{q}^{\prime}-\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}}\Gamma_{\bar{q}^{\prime}-\bar{n}})^{-1}\right]\,.

(2.31)

Let
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
be any time and
\Omega\subset\mathbb{T}^{3}
be a convex set of diameter at most
d(\bar{q}^{\prime},\bar{q}^{\prime\prime})
. Let
i
be such that
\Omega\times\{t_{0}\}\cap\mathrm{supp\,}\psi_{i,\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}\neq\emptyset
.
Let
\Phi_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}
be the flow map such that

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\Phi_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}+\left(\widehat{u}_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}\cdot\nabla\right)\Phi_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}=0\\
\Phi_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}(t_{0},x)=x\,.\end{cases}

We define
\Omega(t)=\Phi_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}(t)^{-1}(\Omega)
.
8
8

8

For any set
\Omega^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{T}^{3}
,
\Phi_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}(t)^{-1}(\Omega^{\prime})=\{x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}:\Phi_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}(t,x)\in\Omega^{\prime}\}
. We shall also sometimes use the notation
\Omega\circ\Phi_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}(t)
.

Then there exists a set
9
9

9

Heuristically this set is
\cup_{t}\mathrm{supp}_{x}\widehat{w}_{\bar{q}^{\prime}}(\cdot,t)\cap\Omega(t)
, but in order to ensure that
(\partial_{t}+\widehat{u}_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{1}_{L}\equiv 0
,
L
does not include any “time cutoffs” which turn pipes on and off.

L=L(\bar{q}^{\prime},\bar{q}^{\prime\prime},\Omega,{t_{0}})\subseteq\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}
such that for all
t\in(t_{0}-\tau_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}\Gamma_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}^{-i+2},t_{0}+\tau_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}\Gamma_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}^{-i+2})
,

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+\widehat{u}_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{1}_{L}(t,\cdot)\equiv 0\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\mathrm{supp\,}_{x}\widehat{w}_{\bar{q}^{\prime}}(x,t)\cap\Omega(t)\subseteq L\cap\{t\}\,.

(2.32)

Furthermore, there exists a finite family of Lipschitz curves
\{\ell_{j,L}\}_{j=1}^{\mathcal{C}_{D}}
of length at most
2d(\bar{q}^{\prime},\bar{q}^{\prime\prime})
which satisfy

L\cap\{t=t_{0}\}\subseteq\bigcup_{j=1}^{\mathcal{C}_{D}}B\left(\ell_{j,L},3\lambda_{\bar{q}^{\prime}}^{-1}\right)\,.

(2.33)

Remark 2.7

(
Segments of deformed pipes of thickness
\lambda_{\bar{q}^{\prime}}^{-1}
).

We will refer to a
3\lambda_{\bar{q}^{\prime}}^{-1}
neighborhood of a Lipschitz curve of length at most
2(\lambda_{\bar{q}^{\prime}-\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}}\Gamma_{\bar{q}^{\prime}-\bar{n}})^{-1}
as a “segment of deformed pipe;” see Definition
4.4
. Since
(\lambda_{\bar{q}^{\prime}-\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}}\Gamma_{\bar{q}^{\prime}-\bar{n}})^{-1}
will be the scale to which our high-frequency pipes will be periodized, Hypothesis
2.6
then asserts that at each step of the iteration, our algorithm can use at most a finite number of high-frequency pipe segments inside any single periodic cell.

Hypothesis 2.8

(
Stress dodging).

For all
k,q^{\prime\prime}
such that
q\leq q^{\prime\prime}\leq k-1
and
q\leq k\leq q+\bar{n}-1
, we assume that

B\left(\mathrm{supp\,}\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime\prime}},\lambda_{q^{\prime\prime}}^{-1}\Gamma_{q^{\prime\prime}+1}\right)\cap\mathrm{supp\,}R_{q}^{k,l}=\emptyset\,.

(2.34)

Hypothesis 2.9

(
Pressure dodging).

We assume that for all
q<k\leq q+\bar{n}-1
,
k\leq k^{\prime}
, and
N+M\leq 2\mathsf{N}_{\rm ind}
,

\displaystyle\left|\psi_{i,k-1}D^{N}D_{t,k-1}^{M}\left(\widehat{w}_{k}\pi_{q}^{k^{\prime}}\right)\right|

\displaystyle<\Gamma_{q}{\Gamma_{k}^{-100}}\left(\pi_{q}^{k}\right)^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}r_{k}^{-1}\Lambda_{k}^{N}\mathcal{M}\left(M,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{k-1}^{i+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1}}\tau_{k-1}^{-1},\Gamma_{k}^{-1}\mathrm{T}_{k}^{-1}\right)\,.

(2.35a)

2.6 Inductive velocity bounds

In this section, we present inductive, local
L^{\infty}
-bounds for velocity increments and total velocity, which are derived from the construction of velocity cutoffs. Additionally, we introduce velocity increment potentials, which express velocity increments as the
{\mathsf{d}}^{\rm th}
divergence of the velocity increment tensor potential, up to a small homogeneous error. This representation will be useful to deal with the pressure current error (see subsection
6.4
for more details). All inductive assumptions in subsection
2.6
except for (
2.44
) at
q\mapsto q+1
have been verified in
[
25
, sections 9, 10]
(see Proposition
3.25
), and we prove (
2.44
) for
q\mapsto q+1
in Lemma
6.8
.

2.6.1 Velocities and velocity increments

In this subsection, we assume that
0\leq q^{\prime}\leq q+\bar{n}-1
. First, for
0\leq i\leq{i_{\rm max}}
,
k\geq 1
,
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, we assume that

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\prod_{l=1}^{k}D^{\alpha_{l}}D_{t,q^{\prime}-1}^{\beta_{l}}\right)\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathrm{supp\,}\psi_{i,q^{\prime}})}\leq\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i+2}\delta_{q^{\prime}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}r_{q^{\prime}-\bar{n}}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}(\lambda_{q^{\prime}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}})^{|\alpha|}\mathcal{M}\left(|\beta|,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{{q^{\prime}}}^{i+3}\tau_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1},\Gamma_{{q^{\prime}-1}}\mathrm{T}_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}\right)

(2.36)

for
|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq{\nicefrac{{3\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}}}{{2}}+1}
. We also assume that for
N\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\left\|D^{N}\Big(\prod_{l=1}^{k}D_{q^{\prime}}^{\alpha_{l}}D_{t,q^{\prime}-1}^{\beta_{l}}\Big)\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathrm{supp\,}\psi_{i,q^{\prime}})}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq(\Gamma_{{q^{\prime}}}^{i+2}\delta_{q^{\prime}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}r_{q^{\prime}-\bar{n}}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}})^{|\alpha|+1}(\lambda_{q^{\prime}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}})^{N+|\alpha|}\mathcal{M}\left(|\beta|,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i+3}\tau_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}\mathrm{T}_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}\right)

(2.37a)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i+2}\delta_{q^{\prime}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}r_{q^{\prime}-\bar{n}}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}(\lambda_{q^{\prime}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}})^{N}(\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i-5}\tau_{q^{\prime}}^{-1})^{|\alpha|}\mathcal{M}\left(|\beta|,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i+3}\tau_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}\mathrm{T}_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}\right)

(2.37b)

whenever
N+|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq{\nicefrac{{3\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}}}{{2}}+1}
. Next, we assume

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\prod_{l=1}^{k}D^{\alpha_{l}}D_{t,q^{\prime}}^{\beta_{l}}\right)D\widehat{u}_{q^{\prime}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathrm{supp\,}\psi_{i,q^{\prime}})}\leq\tau_{q^{\prime}}^{-1}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i-4}(\lambda_{q^{\prime}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}})^{|\alpha|}\mathcal{M}\left(|\beta|,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i-5}\tau_{q^{\prime}}^{-1},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}\mathrm{T}_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}\right)

(2.38)

for
|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq{\nicefrac{{3\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}}}{{2}}}
. In addition, we assume the lossy bounds

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\prod_{l=1}^{k}D^{\alpha_{l}}D_{t,q^{\prime}}^{\beta_{l}}\right)\widehat{u}_{q^{\prime}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathrm{supp\,}\psi_{i,q^{\prime}})}

\displaystyle\leq\tau_{q^{\prime}}^{-1}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i+2}\lambda_{q^{\prime}}(\lambda_{q^{\prime}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}})^{|\alpha|}\mathcal{M}\left(|\beta|,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i-5}\tau_{q^{\prime}}^{-1},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}\mathrm{T}_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}\right)

(2.39a)

\displaystyle\left\|D^{|\alpha|}\partial_{t}^{|\beta|}\widehat{u}_{q^{\prime}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{q^{\prime}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\Lambda_{q}^{|\alpha|}\mathrm{T}_{q^{\prime}}^{-|\beta|}\,,

(2.39b)

hold, where the first bounds holds for
|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq\nicefrac{{3\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}}}{{2}}+1
, and the second bound holds for
|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq 2\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}
.

Remark 2.10

(
Upgrading material derivatives for velocity and velocity cutoffs).

We recall from
[
25
, Remark 2.9]
that we have the bound

\displaystyle\left\|D^{N}D_{t,q^{\prime}}^{M}\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathrm{supp\,}\psi_{i,q^{\prime}})}\lesssim\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i+2}\delta_{q^{\prime}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}r_{q^{\prime}-\bar{n}}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}(\lambda_{q^{\prime}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}})^{N}\mathcal{M}\left(M,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i-5}\tau_{q^{\prime}}^{-1},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}\mathrm{T}_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}\right)

(2.40)

for all
N+M\leq{\nicefrac{{3\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}}}{{2}}+1}
.
We also have
that for all
N+M\leq\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}
,

\displaystyle\frac{{\bf 1}_{\mathrm{supp\,}\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}}}{\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}^{1-(N+M)/\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}}}\left|D^{N}D_{t,q^{\prime}}^{M}\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}(\lambda_{q^{\prime}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}})^{N}\mathcal{M}\left(M,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}}-\mathsf{N}_{\rm cut,t},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i-5}\tau_{q^{\prime}}^{-1},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}\mathrm{T}_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle<\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}(\lambda_{q^{\prime}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}})^{N}\mathcal{M}\left(M,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i-4}\tau_{q^{\prime}}^{-1},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}^{2}\mathrm{T}_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}\right)\,.

(2.41)

2.6.2 Velocity increment potentials

We assume that for all
q-1<q^{\prime}\leq q+\bar{n}-1
and
\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}}
as in (
2.9
), there exists a velocity increment potential

\widehat{\upsilon}_{q^{\prime}}
and an error
\widehat{e}_{q^{\prime}}
such that
\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}}
can be decomposed as
10
10

10

See (
xi
) of subsection
7.1
for the definition of
{\mathsf{d}}
.

\displaystyle\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}}=\mathrm{div}^{{\mathsf{d}}}\widehat{\upsilon}_{q^{\prime}}+\widehat{e}_{q^{\prime}}\,,

(2.42)

which written component-wise gives
\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}}^{\bullet}=\partial_{i_{1}}\cdots\partial_{i_{\mathsf{d}}}\widehat{\upsilon}_{q^{\prime}}^{(\bullet,i_{1},\cdots,i_{\mathsf{d}})}+\widehat{e}_{q^{\prime}}^{\bullet}
. Next, we assume that
\widehat{\upsilon}_{q^{\prime}}
and
\widehat{e}_{q^{\prime}}
satisfy

\displaystyle B\left(\mathrm{supp\,}(\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime\prime}}),\frac{1}{4}\lambda_{q^{\prime\prime}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime\prime}}^{2}\right)\cap\left(\mathrm{supp\,}(\widehat{\upsilon}_{q^{\prime}})\cup\mathrm{supp\,}(\widehat{e}_{q^{\prime}})\right)=\emptyset

(2.43)

for any
q+1\leq q^{\prime\prime}<q^{\prime}
. In addition, we assume that
\widehat{\upsilon}_{q^{\prime},k}^{\bullet}:=\lambda_{q^{\prime}}^{{\mathsf{d}}-k}\partial_{i_{1}}\cdots\partial_{i_{k}}\widehat{\upsilon}_{q^{\prime}}^{(\bullet,i_{1},\dots,i_{\mathsf{d}})}
,
0\leq k\leq{\mathsf{d}}
, satisfies the pointwise estimates

\displaystyle\left|\psi_{i,q^{\prime}-1}D^{N}D_{t,q^{\prime}-1}^{M}\widehat{\upsilon}_{q^{\prime},k}\right|<\Gamma_{q}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}\left(\pi_{q}^{q^{\prime}}\right)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}r_{q^{\prime}-\bar{n}}^{-1}(\lambda_{q^{\prime}}{\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}})^{N}\mathcal{M}\left(M,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}^{i}\tau_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1},\mathrm{T}_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}^{2}\right)

(2.44)

for
N+M\leq\nicefrac{{3\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}}}{{2}}
. Finally, we assume that for
N+M\leq\nicefrac{{3\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}}}{{2}}
,
\widehat{e}_{q^{\prime}}
satisfies the estimates

\displaystyle\left\|D^{N}D_{t,q^{\prime}-1}^{M}\widehat{e}_{q^{\prime}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\delta_{q^{\prime}+2\bar{n}}^{3}\mathrm{T}_{q^{\prime}}^{5\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}}}\lambda_{q^{\prime}}^{-10}(\lambda_{q^{\prime}}{\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}})^{N}\mathcal{M}\left(M,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\tau_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1},\mathrm{T}_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}^{2}\right)\,.

(2.45)

2.7 Inductive proposition and the proof of Theorem
1.1

In this section, we introduce the inductive proposition and give a proof of the main theorem.

Proposition 2.11

(
Inductive proposition).

Fix
\beta\in(0,\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}})
, and choose
\bar{n}
satisfying (
2.1
),
b\in(1,\nicefrac{{25}}{{24}})
satisfying (
2.2
),
T>0
, and a continuous positive function
E(t,x)\geq 0
. Then there exist parameters
\varepsilon_{\Gamma}
,
\mathsf{C_{\infty}}
,
\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{pr}}
,
\mathsf{N}_{\rm cut,t}
,
\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}}
,
\mathsf{N}_{\rm ind}
,
\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}
, depending only on
\beta
,
b
, and
\bar{n}
(see Section
7.1
) such that
we can find sufficiently large
a_{*}=a_{*}(b,\beta,\bar{n},T)
such that for
a\geq a_{*}(b,\beta,\bar{n},T)
, the following statements hold for any
q\geq 0
. Suppose that an approximate solution
(u_{q},p_{q},R_{q},\varphi_{q},-\pi_{q})
of the Euler-Reynolds system (
2.7
) and the relaxed local energy identity (
2.8
) with dissipation measure
E
on the time interval
[-\tau_{q-1},T+\tau_{q-1}]
is given, and suppose that there exists a partition of unity
\{\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}^{6}\}_{i\geq 0}
of
[-\tau_{q-1},T+\tau_{q-1}]\times\mathbb{T}^{3}
for
q-1\leq q^{\prime}\leq q+\bar{n}-1
such that

•

\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}
satisfies (
2.13
)–(
2.19
), and

•

the velocity
u_{q}
and the errors
R_{q}
,
\varphi_{q}
, and
\pi_{q}
may be decomposed as in (
2.9
)–(
2.12
) so that
(
2.20
)–(
2.29
), Hypotheses
2.5
–Hypothesis
2.9

2.36
)–(
2.39
), and (
2.42
)–(
2.45
) hold.

Then there exist a new partition of unity
\{\psi_{i,q+\bar{n}}^{6}\}_{i\geq 0}
of
[-\tau_{q},T+\tau_{q}]\times\mathbb{T}^{3}
satisfying (
2.13
)–(
2.19
) for
q^{\prime}=q+\bar{n}
, and a new approximate solution
(u_{q+1},p_{q+1},R_{q+1},\varphi_{q+1},-\pi_{q+1})
satisfying (
2.7
) and (
2.8
) on
[-\tau_{q},T+\tau_{q}]
with dissipation measure
E
satisfying the following conditions. The approximate solution may be decomposed as in (
2.9
)–(
2.12
) for
q\mapsto q+1
so that (
2.20
)–(
2.29
) and Hypothesis
2.5
–Hypothesis
2.9
hold for
q\mapsto q+1
, and (
2.36
)–(
2.39
) and (
2.42
)–(
2.45
) hold for
q\mapsto q+1
.

Assuming for the moment that Proposition
2.11
holds, we prove Theorem
1.1
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Let
\upbeta\in(0,\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}})
be fixed as in Theorem
1.1
. Since Proposition
2.11
holds for
\beta\in(0,\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}})
, we can choose
\beta\in(\upbeta,\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}})
,
\bar{n}
large, and
b
close enough to
1
such that

\displaystyle\upbeta<\beta-(b-1)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,,\quad\frac{1}{1-3\upbeta}<\beta b^{3\bar{n}}\left(\left[(b-1)\left((b^{\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}-1}+\dots+b+1)^{2}+2000b^{\bar{n}}\right)+\frac{4b^{\bar{n}-1}}{1+\dots+b^{\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}-1}}\right]\right)^{-1}\,,

(2.46)

and (
2.1
) and (
2.2
) hold. Let
a_{*}=a_{*}(b,\beta,\bar{n},T)
be as in Proposition
2.11
, fix
a\geq a_{*}
, and define

\displaystyle\Gamma_{q}=1\quad\text{for }-\bar{n}\leq q\leq-1\,,\qquad r_{q}=\begin{cases}\lambda_{q+\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q+\bar{n}}^{-1}&\text{for }-\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}\leq q<0\\
\lambda_{0}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{\bar{n}}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}&\text{for }-\bar{n}\leq q<-\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}\,.\end{cases}

Step 1: Construction of the initial approximate solution
(u_{0},p_{0},R_{0},\varphi_{0},-\pi_{0})
on
[-1,T+1]
.

We first define

\displaystyle\widehat{u}_{-1}=0\,,\quad\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{for $0\leq q^{\prime}\leq\bar{n}-2$}\,,\qquad p_{0}=\varphi_{0}=0\,,\qquad\pi_{0}^{k}=\Gamma_{k}\delta_{k+\bar{n}}\,,

\displaystyle\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1&i=0\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\quad\text{for }0\leq q^{\prime}\leq\bar{n}-1\,,

so that

\displaystyle\widehat{u}_{q^{\prime}}=0\quad\text{for }0\leq q^{\prime}\leq\bar{n}-2\,,\qquad\pi_{0}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Gamma_{k}\delta_{k+\bar{n}}\,.

Let
\bar{\vartheta}_{e_{3},\lambda_{\bar{n}-1},r_{-1}}
and
\bar{\varrho}_{e_{3},\lambda_{\bar{n}-1},r_{-1}}
be
smooth functions such that they depend only on the
x_{1},x_{2}
variables, are
\left(\nicefrac{{\mathbb{T}^{3}}}{{\lambda_{\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}-1}\Gamma_{-1})}}\right)
-periodic, have support contained in pipes of thickness
\lambda_{\bar{n}-1}^{-1}
, and satisfy

\displaystyle\bar{\varrho}_{e_{3},\lambda_{\bar{n}-1},r_{-1}}=\lambda_{\bar{n}-1}^{-{\mathsf{d}}}\mathrm{div}^{\mathsf{d}}\bar{\vartheta}_{e_{3},\lambda_{\bar{n}-1},r_{-1}},\quad\left\|\nabla^{n}\bar{\vartheta}_{e_{3},\lambda_{\bar{n}-1},r_{-1}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}+\left\|\nabla^{n}\bar{\varrho}_{e_{3},\lambda_{\bar{n}-1},r_{-1}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C_{0}\lambda_{\bar{n}-1}^{n}r_{-1}^{-1}

for all
n\leq 2\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}+{\mathsf{d}}
and some positive constant
C_{0}=C_{0}(2\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin},{\mathsf{d}})
. To lighten notation, we abbreviate
\bar{\varrho}_{e_{3},\lambda_{\bar{n}-1},r_{-1}}
and
\bar{\vartheta}_{e_{3},\lambda_{\bar{n}-1},r_{-1}}
by
\rho_{0}
and
\theta_{0}
, respectively.
We then define

\displaystyle u_{0}

\displaystyle=\widehat{w}_{\bar{n}-1}=e(t)\rho_{0}(x_{1},x_{2})e_{3},\quad e(t):=\Gamma_{\bar{n}}^{-100}\delta_{2\bar{n}}r_{-1}\exp(-\tau_{0}^{-1}(t+1))

\displaystyle R_{0}

\displaystyle=e^{\prime}(t)\begin{pmatrix}0&0&(\lambda_{\bar{n}-1}^{-{\mathsf{d}}}\mathrm{div}^{{\mathsf{d}}-1}\theta_{0})_{1}\\
0&0&(\lambda_{\bar{n}-1}^{-{\mathsf{d}}}\mathrm{div}^{{\mathsf{d}}-1}\theta_{0})_{2}\\
(\lambda_{\bar{n}-1}^{-{\mathsf{d}}}\mathrm{div}^{{\mathsf{d}}-1}\theta_{0})_{1}&(\lambda_{\bar{n}-1}^{-{\mathsf{d}}}\mathrm{div}^{{\mathsf{d}}-1}\theta_{0})_{2}&0\end{pmatrix}=R_{0}^{\bar{n}-1,l}\,,

where
(g)_{k}
denotes the
k^{\rm th}
component of the vector
g
. Notice that
R_{0}
is constructed to satisfy
\partial_{t}u_{0}=\mathrm{div}R_{0}
.

From the construction, we have

\mathrm{div}u_{0}=\mathrm{div}(|u_{0}|^{2}u_{0})=0
and
\mathrm{div}(u_{0}\otimes u_{0})=0
, so that one can easily see that
(u_{0},p_{0},R_{0},\varphi_{0},-\pi_{0})
satisfies the Euler-Reynolds system (
2.7
) and the relaxed local energy identity (
2.8
) with
E(t,x):=-e(t)e^{\prime}(t)|\rho_{0}|^{2}

on the time interval
[-1,T+1]
. We will now check that the constructed approximate solution and the partitions of unity satisfy the remaining inductive assumptions appearing in Section (
2.2
)–(
2.6
) on
[-1,T+1]
.

We first note that for
0\leq q^{\prime}\leq\bar{n}-1
, it is immediate that
\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}
satisfies (
2.13
)–(
2.19
). Next, letting
R_{0}^{k}=0
for
0\leq k\leq\bar{n}-2
,
R_{0}^{\bar{n}-1}=R_{0}^{\bar{n}-1,l}
, and
\varphi_{0}^{k}=0
for
0\leq k\leq\bar{n}-1
,
we have the decompositions (
2.9
)–(
2.12
). Using the convention
B(A,r)=\emptyset
for the empty set
A
and setting
\widehat{\upsilon}_{\bar{n}-1}=e(t)\theta_{0}e_{3}
,
\widehat{\upsilon}_{q^{\prime}}=e_{q^{\prime\prime}}=0
for
0\leq q^{\prime}\leq\bar{n}-2
and
0\leq q^{\prime\prime}\leq\bar{n}-1
, we can easily verify (
2.20
)–(
2.29
), Hypotheses
2.5
,
2.8
, and
2.9
, (
2.36
), (
2.37
), and (
2.42
)–(
2.45
) for
q=0
. As for Hypothesis
2.6
, it is enough to consider
q^{\prime}=\bar{n}-1
. Since
q^{\prime\prime}<q=\bar{n}-1
and
i
needs to be
0
, recalling that
\widehat{u}_{q^{\prime\prime}}=0
, we have
\Phi_{q^{\prime\prime}}(t,x)=x
and hence
\Omega(t)=\Omega
. Then, (
2.32
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\mathrm{supp\,}\widehat{w}_{\bar{n}-1}\cap\Omega\subset L\cap\Omega\,.

Therefore, we choose
L
as the collection of the
(\nicefrac{{\mathbb{T}}}{{\lambda_{\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}-1}\Gamma_{\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}-1}}})^{3}
-periodic pipes of thickness
\lambda_{\bar{n}-1}^{-1}
containing the support of
\widehat{w}_{\bar{n}-1}
, which verifies the hypothesis. Lastly, considering (
2.38
) and (
2.39
), it is enough to prove it when
q^{\prime}=\bar{n}-1
and
i=0
. Since we have
\widehat{u}_{\bar{n}-1}=\widehat{w}_{\bar{n}-1}
and

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\prod_{l=1}^{k}D^{\alpha_{l}}\partial_{t}^{\beta_{l}}\right)\widehat{w}_{\bar{n}-1}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}\leq C_{0}\Gamma_{\bar{n}}^{-100}\delta_{2\bar{n}}\lambda_{\bar{n}-1}^{|\alpha|}\tau_{0}^{-|\beta|}\,,

applying Lemma
A.2
to
p=\infty
,
v=0
,
w=\widehat{w}_{\bar{n}-1}
,
\Omega=\mathbb{T}^{3}
,
N_{*}=\nicefrac{{7\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}}}{{4}}
, we obtain (
2.38
) and (
2.39
).

Step 2: From Proposition
2.11
to Theorem
1.1
.
In
Step 1
, we checked that the inductive assumptions hold at the base case
q=0
of the induction. We now apply Proposition
2.11
inductively with
E(t,x)=-e(t)e^{\prime}(t)|\rho_{0}|^{2}
to produce a sequence of approximate solutions
(u_{q},p_{q},R_{q},\varphi_{q},-\pi_{q})
such that all inductive assumptions hold for all
q\geq 0
. In order to compute the Besov norm of the limiting solution, we will use the inductive bound (
2.18
) for
\psi_{i,q}
, the bounds (
2.36
) for
\widehat{w}_{q}
and
\nabla\widehat{w}_{q}
, and the
q
-independent bound in (
2.14
) for
{i_{\rm max}}
. Then by the definition of
B^{\beta^{\prime}}_{3,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
in (
1.7
), interpolation, and the standard characterization of
W^{1,3}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
in terms of difference quotients, we have that

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{w}_{q}\right\|_{C^{0}B^{\beta^{\prime}}_{3,\infty}}\leq\left\|\nabla\widehat{w}_{q}\right\|_{C^{0}W^{1,3}}^{\beta^{\prime}}\left\|\widehat{w}_{q}\right\|_{C^{0}L^{3}}^{(1-\beta^{\prime})}\lesssim\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\Gamma_{q}^{\frac{{\mathsf{C}_{b}}}{3}+3}r_{q-\bar{n}}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}\lambda_{q}^{\beta^{\prime}}\leq\lambda_{q}^{-\beta+(b-1)\varepsilon_{\Gamma}\left(\frac{6+b}{b-1}+3\right)+\frac{1}{3}\left(\frac{b^{\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}}-1}{b^{\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}}}\right)+\beta^{\prime}}\,.

(2.47)

Using that
0<\varepsilon_{\Gamma}\ll(b-1)^{2}
(see immediately below (
2.4
)) and
(b^{\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}-1}+\dots+b+1)(b-1)<(b-1)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
(from (
2.2b
)), we have that the exponent for
\lambda_{q}
in (
2.47
) is no larger than
-\beta+(b-1)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}+\beta^{\prime}
. Now from (
2.46
), we have that the series
\sum_{q\geq\bar{n}}\widehat{w}_{q}
is absolutely summable in
C^{0}_{t}B^{\upbeta}_{3,\infty}
. Therefore the limiting velocity field
u=u_{0}+\sum_{q\geq\bar{n}}\widehat{w}_{q}\in C^{0}_{t}B^{\upbeta}_{3,\infty}
, as desired. Since
R_{q},\pi_{q}\to 0
in
C^{0}_{t}L^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}
, we obtain from the equation
-\Delta p_{q}=\mathrm{div}\mathrm{div}(u_{q}\otimes u_{q}+\pi_{q}\mathrm{Id}-R_{q})
that the limiting pressure
p\in C^{0}_{t}L^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}
. Using in addition that
\varphi_{q}\to 0
in
C^{0}_{t}L^{1}
, the limiting pair
(u,p)
therefore solves the Euler equations (
1.1
) and satisfies

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left(\frac{1}{2}|u|^{2}\right)+\mathrm{div}\left(\left(\frac{1}{2}|u|^{2}+p\right)u\right)=e(t)e^{\prime}(t)|\rho_{0}|^{2}\leq 0

in the sense of distributions. In particular, the strict local energy inequality holds in the interior of
\mathrm{supp\,}(\rho_{0})
, which leads to the total kinetic energy dissipation.

In order to conclude the proof of the theorem, we only need to show that
u\in C^{0}_{t}L^{\frac{1}{(1-3\upbeta)}}
. Using the
L^{3}
and
L^{\infty}
bounds on
\widehat{w}_{q}
provided by (
2.26c
) and (
2.20
), we sum over
0\leq i\leq{i_{\rm max}}(q)
as before, arriving at

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{w}_{q}\right\|_{L^{3}}\leq C\delta_{q+\bar{n}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}r_{q-\bar{n}}^{-1}\Gamma_{q}^{5}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\left\|\widehat{w}_{q}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\Gamma_{q}^{\nicefrac{{\mathsf{C_{\infty}}}}{{2}}+5}r_{q-\bar{n}}^{-1}\,,

where the constant
C
depends only on our upper bound for
{i_{\rm max}}(q)
, and so only on
\beta
and
b
through (
2.14
). Using interpolation, the definition (
7.2
) of
\mathsf{C_{\infty}}
,
\varepsilon_{\Gamma}\ll(b-1)^{2}
, and the above established bounds, for
p\in[3,\infty)
we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{w}_{q}\right\|_{L^{p}}\leq\left\|\widehat{w}_{q}\right\|_{L^{3}}^{\frac{3}{p}}\left\|\widehat{w}_{q}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}^{1-\frac{3}{p}}

\displaystyle\leq C\delta_{q+\bar{n}}^{\frac{3}{2p}}\Gamma_{q}^{\mathsf{C_{\infty}}{\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{3}{2p}\right)}}\Gamma_{q}^{5}r_{q-\bar{n}}^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq C\lambda_{q-\bar{n}}^{\frac{3}{2p}(-2\beta b^{2\bar{n}})+\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{3}{2p}\right)6b^{\bar{n}}\left[(b-1)(b^{\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}-1}+\dots+b+1)^{2}+2000(b-1)b^{\bar{n}}+\frac{4b^{\bar{n}-1}}{1+\dots+b^{\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}-1}}\right]}\,,

(2.48)

where the constant
C=C(\beta,b)\geq 1
. In order to ensure that the exponent of
\lambda_{q-\bar{n}}
on the right side of (
2.48
) is strictly negative, a short computation shows that it will suffice to choose

\displaystyle p<p_{*}(\beta,b)=:\beta b^{2\bar{n}}\left(b^{\bar{n}}\left[(b-1)(b^{\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}-1}+\dots+b+1)^{2}+2000(b-1)b^{\bar{n}}+\frac{4b^{\bar{n}-1}}{1+\dots+b^{\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}-1}}\right]\right)^{-1}\,.

Then by (
2.46
), we can choose
p=(1-3\upbeta)^{-1}
, and so
u\in C^{0}_{t}L^{\frac{1}{1-3\upbeta}}
.
∎

3
Convex integration set-up and preliminary results

The first goal of this section is to set up the proof of Proposition
2.11
by defining a premollified velocity increment
w_{q+1}
. The second goal is to record lemmas and estimates related to the creation of new Euler-Reynolds stress errors. We do not give the full proofs of these results, opting instead to give a few ideas when necessary and refer to
[
25
]
for further details.

First, we will define a premollified velocity increment (still only heuristically, and not with the final notation) by

\displaystyle w_{q+1}=w_{q+1,\varphi}+w_{q+1,R}\approx\sum_{j}a_{j}(\nabla\widehat{u}_{q},\varphi_{q}^{q})\mathbb{B}_{j,\varphi}\circ\Phi_{q,j}+\sum_{j^{\prime}}a_{j^{\prime}}(\nabla\widehat{u}_{q},\varphi_{q}^{q},R_{q}^{q})\mathbb{B}_{{j^{\prime}},R}\circ\Phi_{q,{j^{\prime}}}\,.

(3.1)

The intermittent Mikado bundles
\mathbb{B}_{j,\bullet}
are defined in subsection
3.1
. Then in subsection
3.2
, we define all the components of the coefficient functions
a_{\bullet}
. These components include both non-inductive cutoffs, which serve to partition time, measure the level sets of the intermittent pressure and its derivatives, and localize the supports of the bundles, and the inductive cutoffs, which partition the level sets of the gradient of the velocity field. The flow maps
\Phi_{q,j}
are then defined and estimated on the intersection of the supports of velocity and time cutoffs. Estimates for the cutoff functions are record throughout, and “aggregation lemmas” which serve to combine local pointwise bounds into global
L^{p}
bounds are given in subsubsection
3.2.7
. With these ingredients in hand, we define and estimate the premollified velocity increment precisely in subsection
3.3
. We also define and estimate the associated pressure increments and current errors created by these pressure increments, as well as the velocity increment potentials, which are necessary for example in Lemma
6.13
. Then sections
3.4
–
3.6
recall the definitions, estimates, and associated pressure increments of new stress errors, new transport and Nash current errors, and new mollification current errors, respectively.

3.1 Intermittent Mikado bundles

In this subsection, we introduce the intermittent Mikado bundles

\mathbb{B}_{{(\xi)},\diamond}=\boldsymbol{\rho}_{(\xi)}^{\diamond}\sum_{I}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{\xi}^{I,\diamond}\mathbb{W}_{{(\xi)},\diamond}^{I}\,\quad\diamond=R,\varphi\,.

(3.2)

The scalar function
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{(\xi)}^{\diamond}
is a mildly intermittent (meaning that in any
L^{p}
-estimates we can simply appeal to its
L^{\infty}
norm) “bundling pipe” defined in Proposition
3.1
. The notation
\diamond
distinguishes objects used to correct stress errors
R
from objects used to correct current errors
\varphi
. We put
\diamond
in the exponent of
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{(\xi)}^{\diamond}
since
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{(\xi)}^{\diamond}
is defined as the second or third power of a master function. The vector
\xi
is the direction of the velocity field of the bundle and satisfies
\xi\cdot\nabla\mathbb{B}_{{(\xi)},\diamond}\equiv 0
. We use the notation
{(\xi)}
as a short-hand for the numerous indices on which the placement of the bundling pipe will depend; these indices will be detailed in Section
4
when we carry out the placement. The direction vectors
\xi
are chosen to belong to sets
\Xi,\Xi^{\prime}\subset(\mathbb{S}^{2}\cap\mathbb{Q}^{3})
, both of which are used in standard geometric lemmas (
[
25
, Propositions 4.1 and 4.2]
) to engender symmetric tensors or vectors as “positive convex integrals” of the simple tensors and vectors, respectively, coming from the geometric lemmas. The next components in the bundle are a strongly anisotropic cutoff function
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{\xi}^{I,\diamond}
, which is defined in Definition
3.2
and used to localize the support of the highly intermittent Mikado flows
\mathbb{W}_{(\xi),\diamond}^{I}
, recalled in Propositions
3.3
and
3.4
. The strongly anisotropic cutoff function is indexed by
I
and depends on the vector direction
\xi
, but
not
any further indices like the bundling pipe flow, due to the fact that we do not “place” the strong anisotropic cutoff functions. These cutoffs also satisfy a normalization property depending on
\diamond
; this is used for example to enact the cubic cancellation in subsection
5.2
. Finally, we note that in Section
4
, the intermittent Mikado flow
\mathbb{W}_{(\xi),\diamond}^{I}
will be placed carefully on the intersection of the support of an anistropic cutoff
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{\xi}^{I,\diamond}
indexed by
I
, and the supports of further cutoff functions, which we detail later and depend on indices abbreviated with the notation
{(\xi)}
. While one might guess that the Mikado flows for
\diamond=R,\varphi
are defined as powers of a master flow, in fact certain distinct cancellation properties are needed for
\varphi
and
R
; see for example Proposition
3.3
, item (
5
), which ensures that the
L^{2}
-normalized pipes do not create a low-frequency term in the trilinear current oscillation error in subsection
5.2
.

(
Bundling pipe flows
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\xi,\diamond}^{k}

The master scalar functions

-periodic
and

-periodic,
11
11

11

n_{*}

such that

for all

. We denote by

.

and satisfies

.

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{\xi,k}^{6}=1

Definition 3.2

(
Strongly anisotropic cutoff).

For a given vector direction
\xi
, the set of strongly anisotropic cutoffs
\{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{\xi}^{I}\}_{I}
is
a partition of unity which divides the orthogonal space
\xi^{\perp}\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
into a grid of squares
\mathcal{S}_{I}=\mathcal{S}_{I,\xi}
of side-length
\approx\lambda_{q+\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}}^{-1}
. Each cutoff satisfies

\displaystyle(\xi\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{\xi}=0\,,\quad\left\|\nabla^{N}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{\xi}^{I}\right\|_{\infty}\lesssim\lambda_{q+\nicefrac{{\bar{n}}}{{2}}}^{N}\,\forall N\leq 3\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}\,,\quad\sum_{I}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{\xi}^{I})^{6}=1\,,\quad\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{\xi}^{I}=\begin{cases}1&\textnormal{ on }\frac{3}{4}\mathcal{S}_{I}\\
0&\textnormal{ outside }\frac{5}{4}\mathcal{S}_{I}\end{cases}\,.

(3.4)

We now recall the
L^{2}
-normalized intermittent pipe flows used for the Reynolds corrector
w_{q+1,R}
.

Proposition 3.3

(
Intermittent pipe flows for Reynolds corrector).

The intermittent pipe flows
\mathcal{W}_{\xi,\lambda,r}^{k}
for Reynold correctors defined in
